Nonlinear elasticity of pre-stressed single crystals at high pressure and various elastic moduli


Abstract




Valery I. Levitas

Iowa State University, Departments of Aerospace Engineering and Mechanical Engineering,
Ames, Iowa 50011, USA, vlevitas@iastate.edu;

Ames Laboratory, Division of Materials Science and Engineering, Ames, IA, USA

A general nonlinear theory for the elasticity of pre-stressed single crystals is presented. Various types of elastic moduli are defined, their importance is determined, and relationships between them are presented. In particular,
\boldsymbol{B}
moduli are present in the relationship between the Jaumann objective time derivative of the Cauchy stress and deformation rate and are broadly used in computational algorithms in various finite-element codes. Possible applications to simplified linear solutions for complex nonlinear elasticity problems are outlined and illustrated for a superdislocation. The effect of finite rotations is fully taken into account and analyzed. Different types of the bulk and shear moduli under different constraints are defined and connected to the effective properties of polycrystalline aggregates.
Expressions for elastic energy and stress-strain relationships for small distortions with respect to a pre-stressed configuration are derived in detail. Under initial hydrostatic load, general consistency conditions for elastic moduli and compliances are derived that follow from the existence of the generalized tensorial equation of state under hydrostatic loading obtained from single crystal or polycrystal. It is shown that
\boldsymbol{B}
moduli can be found from the expression for the Gibbs energy. However, higher-order elastic moduli defined from the Gibbs energy do not have any meaning since they do not directly participate in any known equations, like stress-strain relationships and wave propagation equation.
The deviatoric projection of
\boldsymbol{B}
can also be found from the expression for the elastic energy for isochoric small strain increments, and the missing components of
\boldsymbol{B}
can be found from the consistency conditions. Numerous inconsistencies and errors in the known works are analyzed.

1
INTRODUCTION

Elastic moduli under high pressures play a fundamental role for the solution of numerous basic and applied problems. These problems include the determination of tensorial stress-strain relationships for single crystal and polycrystalline aggregates,
which are utilized for continuum simulations of deformation of material under extreme dynamic
[
1
,
2
,
3
]
or static
[
4
,
5
]
loadings.
Nonlinearity of the elasticity rules lead
to elastic instabilities

[
6
,
7
,
8
,
9
,
10
]
.
They cause various physical phenomena, including phase transitions (PT, i.e., crystal-crystal
[
11
,
12
,
13
,
14
,
15
]
, amorphization
[
16
,
17
,
18
,
19
,
20
]
, and melting
[
21
,
22
]
), slip
[
23
]
, twinning
[
24
]
, and different fracture modes
[
10
,
9
,
25
,
26
]
.

A quite general and correct description of nonlinear elasticity in terms of elastic moduli with respect to the reference configuration under arbitrary stress tensor was presented in
[
27
,
28
,
29
,
30
,
31
]
. While in
[
27
,
28
]
the presentation was heavily mixed with atomic treatment, in
[
29
]
and then
[
30
,
31
]
elastic moduli
\boldsymbol{B}
are introduced that determined
relationship between small Cauchy stress increment and strain with respect to the current configuration, which played the major role in the determination of elastic lattice instability under homogeneous perturbations in
[
7
,
8
,
9
,
10
]
.

Unfortunately, in the most popular for this topic textbook
[
6
]
derivation of the elastic moduli
\boldsymbol{B}
from the Cauchy (true) stress - small strain relationship is very confusing
because it is based on small strain increment from the intermediate reference configuration with respect to stress-free configuration (instead of from the intermediate to the current configuration) and corresponding linearization. In
[
29
]
stress-free reference configuration is not introduced at all; that is why the relationship between elastic moduli in the current configuration
\bar{\boldsymbol{C}}
and the all-rank elastic constant tensors
\boldsymbol{C}_{0}^{n}
of the undeformed crystal are not presented. This, however, was elaborated in
[
30
]
.

It was suggested in
[
32
]
for cubic crystals under initial hydrostatic pressure to use the second derivatives of the enthalpy (instead of Helmholtz energy) with respect to the Lagrangian
strain tensor to determine elastic moduli, claiming that they play the same role under pressure as usual moduli for unstressed solid. However, justification was not given, and as we will discuss, this is not true for the third- and higher-order elastic moduli.
This direction was further elaborated in terms of the Gibbs energy and applied for atomistic determination of elastic moduli for specific materials in
[
33
,
34
,
35
,
36
,
37
]
. However, as we will show in the paper, such elastic moduli, starting with the third order, do not have any direct physical applications.

One of the methods to determine some of the components of the tensor
\boldsymbol{B}
is based calculation of the elastic energy for the small incremental strain
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
, which is isochoric up to terms
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}^{2}

[
38
,
39
,
40
]
.
However, because justification was not given, this method was criticized in
[
33
]
and
[
41
]
. This led to mutual critics
in
[
42
,
43
]
. It is shown in
[
42
,
8
]
for hexagonal crystals that for several small isochoric strains without rotations, the free energy indeed represents quadratic form with
\boldsymbol{B}
moduli. However, the general proof is lacking.
Also, there is no general consideration for exactly which components of the
\boldsymbol{B}
moduli can be determined with such a method and how to find the remaining components.

While using the density functional theory (e.g.,
[
44
,
45
,
46
]
),
a linear elasticity theory that neglects the effect of initial stresses was used for calculations of elastic moduli as the coefficients in quadratic elastic energy.
However, when the same approaches have been applied for a stressed solid, the effect of prestressing is often neglected.
For example, in
[
47
]
these equations have been applied at high pressures but pressure correction was not mentioned.

Elastic moduli
\boldsymbol{B}
were found directly from the stress-small strain relationship in

[
48
]
for
\alpha
,
\omega
, and
\beta
Zr. One of the general problems is that in most papers, different elastic moduli
\boldsymbol{C}
and
\boldsymbol{B}
are often confused. The importance of a clear definition of which elastic moduli are considered in the problem under study is demonstrated
in
[
49
]
and the following discussion
[
50
,
51
]
.
In
[
49
]
, elastic moduli for
\alpha-
quartz were taken from the linear relationship between the stress and strain tensors, without specifying which stress and strain, and used for evaluation of lattice instability. It was assumed in
[
50
]
that these elastic moduli are
\boldsymbol{C}
and should be pressure corrected
to
\boldsymbol{B}
for the stability study. Then it was explained in
[
51
]
that their elastic moduli were, in fact,
\boldsymbol{B}
, because they came directly from the stress-strain relationship, meaning Cauchy stress-small strain with respect to the deformed state.

Also, as we will show, even the definition of the bulk modulus under nonhydrostatic loading contains ambiguities, which leads to various confusion.

We believe that it is time to revisit in a strict and consistent way, within the framework of modern continuum mechanics, the main expressions for all reasonable elastic moduli, the relationship between them, and rigorous determination methods.
The paper is organized as follows. Section
2
contains finite-strain kinematics, expressions for different stresses and energy, and definitions of elastic moduli in the stress-free (
\boldsymbol{C}_{0}
) and arbitrary reference configurations (
\boldsymbol{C}
) for arbitrary strains, as well as elastic constants of all ranks (
\boldsymbol{C}_{0}^{n}
) in the stress-free configuration for zero strains. Relationships between all these moduli are derived. Section
3
presents relationships between the objective Jaumann rate of the Cauchy stress and deformation rate, which appear to be connected by elastic moduli
\boldsymbol{B}
. The rate of the second Piola-Kirchhoff stress and Lagrangian strain are
connected by elastic moduli
\boldsymbol{C}
.
Possible applications of the rate equations to simplified linear solutions for complex nonlinear elasticity problems are outlined.
Various reasonable definitions of the bulk modulus and compressibility (bulk compliance) under nonhydrostatic stresses and their relationships with
\boldsymbol{B}
moduli are presented in Section
4
.
Approximation for small distortions with respect to a pre-stressed intermediate configuration is described in Section
5
.
Elastic moduli
\tilde{\boldsymbol{C}}
are introduced as coefficients of the quadratic elastic energy in terms of small distortions. They also directly appear in equations of motion and wave propagation. Issues related to the presence of small rotations in the elastic energy and invariance under rigid-body rotations in the current configuration are addressed. An example of simple shear is considered.
Section
6
presents a particular case when the intermediate configuration is under hydrostatic pressure. All equations from the previous Sections are simplified for this case, and some new relationships are derived. Consistency conditions for moduli
\boldsymbol{B}
and corresponding compliances are derived based on the existence of the generalized equation of state under hydrostatic loading.
This is done for the data obtained for single crystal and polycrystalline samples. Relationships are presented between bulk and shear moduli of the isotropic
polycrystal and moduli
\boldsymbol{B}
under pressure. It is proved that the Gibbs energy is a quadratic form in small
strains with moduli
\boldsymbol{B}
. It is also proven that the elastic energy for small isochoric strains is a quadratic form with the deviatoric projection of moduli
\boldsymbol{B}
. The rest of the components of
\boldsymbol{B}
can be determined from the consistency conditions.
Hydrostatic loading and isotropic deformation are obtained through energy minimization in Section
6.4.7
.
Simplifications for isotropic materials and cubic crystals are presented.
The principle of superposition for defects and inelasticity in nonlinear elasticity with application to superdislocation and promotion of phase transformations by dislocation pileups are considered in Section
7
.
Summary of the current work is presented in Section
8
.
Tensor notations used in the paper are presented in Appendix A. Appendix B contains derivation of some equations.
Appendix C describes analysis the invariance under superposed rigid-body rotations in the current
configuration. Appendix D treats a simple shear under hydrostatic pressure.
Analysis of the previous approaches is given in Appendix E.

2
MAIN DEFINITIONS

2.1
Kinematics

Figure 1:
The reference stress-free configuration
\Omega_{0}
, the deformed (current) configuration
\Omega
under the Cauchy stress
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
, and an arbitrary intermediate configuration
\Omega_{*}
with initial stress
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}
.
Multiplicative decomposition of
the deformation gradient
\boldsymbol{F}_{0}=\boldsymbol{F}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}_{*}
is valid.

The motion of an elastic body is presented by a continuous function
{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}}={\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}}({\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}}_{0},t)
,
where
{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}}_{0}
and
r
are the positions of material points in the undeformed (stress-free,
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\mbox{\boldsymbol{$0$}}
)
\Omega_{0}
and the current deformed
\Omega

configurations under the Cauchy (true) stress tensor
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
, respectively;
t
is time.
Let us consider some homogeneously deformed intermediate configuration
\Omega_{*}
described by position vector
{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}}_{*}={\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}}_{*}({\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}}_{0},t)
, which can be arbitrarily chosen depending on the goals. The following multiplicative decomposition of the total deformation gradient
\boldsymbol{F}_{0}
from the reference configuration
\Omega_{0}
to the current configuration
\Omega
is valid:

\boldsymbol{F}_{0}:=\frac{\partial\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}}{\partial\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}_{0}}=\frac{\partial\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}}{\partial\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}_{*}}\cdot\frac{\partial\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}_{*}}{\partial\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}_{0}}=\boldsymbol{F}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}_{*};\hskip 23.49976pt\boldsymbol{F}:=\frac{\partial\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}}{\partial\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}_{*}};\hskip 23.49976pt\boldsymbol{F}_{*}:=\frac{\partial\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}_{*}}{\partial\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}_{0}},

(1)

where
\boldsymbol{F}_{*}
is deformation gradient describing deformation from the reference configuration
\Omega_{0}
to the intermediate configuration
\Omega_{*}
and
\boldsymbol{F}
is deformation gradient describing deformation from the intermediate configuration
\Omega_{*}
to the current configuration
\Omega
.
In the component form

F_{0,ij}:=\frac{\partial r_{i}}{\partial r_{0,j}}=\frac{\partial r_{i}}{\partial r_{*k}}\frac{\partial r_{*k}}{\partial r_{0,j}}=F_{ik}F_{*kj};\hskip 23.49976ptF_{ik}:=\frac{\partial r_{i}}{\partial r_{*k}};\hskip 23.49976ptF_{*kj}:=\frac{\partial r_{*k}}{\partial r_{0,j}}.

(2)

In contrast to the intermediate configurations in elastoplasticity
[
52
,
53
]
and theory of phase transformations
[
54
,
55
]
, which corresponds to the stress-free state after unloading after plastic deformation and phase transformation, the intermediate configuration here can be chosen arbitrarily based on the problem in hands and convenience. For example, to derive relationships for small strains superposed on the finite strains, the intermediate configuration is chosen close to the current one; to derive rate equations, they are infinitesimally close. The intermediate configuration may correspond to the chosen pre-stressed state, particularly hydrostatically pre-stressed, to study the effect of this stress on elastic moduli or wave propagation. Pre-stressing can be heterogeneous, e.g., due to heterogeneous loading or inelastic deformation gradient
\boldsymbol{F}_{in}
, e.g., plastic, thermal, transformational, at others; then the decomposition (
1
) should be substituted with
\boldsymbol{F}_{0}=\boldsymbol{F}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}_{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}_{in}

[
52
,
53
,
54
,
55
]
. In general, the intermediate configuration and multiplicative decomposition can be introduced for each point locally through the field of
\mbox{\boldsymbol{$F$}}_{*}
, which is incompatible, i.e., a continuous vector function
\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}_{*}({\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}}_{0})
does not exist. This is similar to incompatible plastic and transformational deformation gradients in the elastoplasticity
[
52
,
53
]
and the theory of phase transformations
[
54
,
55
]
. For example, when the hydrostatic pressure is much larger than the deviatoric stresses and the elasticity rule is presented in terms of pressure-dependent elastic moduli, then for the heterogeneously loaded body, the intermediate configuration for each point is under current local hydrostatic pressure (i.e., is obtained by local relaxation of the deviatoric stresses) and is generally incompatible. If the field
\boldsymbol{F}_{*}({\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}}_{0})
is associated with the current heterogeneously deformed configuration, it is compatible.

Eq. (
1
) implies for Jacobian determinants

J_{0}:=det\boldsymbol{F}_{0}=JJ_{*};\hskip 23.49976ptJ:=det\boldsymbol{F};\hskip 23.49976ptJ_{*}:=det\boldsymbol{F}_{*},

(3)

where
det
is the determinant of a tensor,
J_{0}
,
J
, and
J_{*}
characterize the volume-ratios for the corresponding deformation gradients.

The Lagrangian total strain
\boldsymbol{E}_{0}
and strains based on
\boldsymbol{F}_{*}
and
\boldsymbol{F}
are defined as

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{E}_{0}=\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{F}_{0}^{T}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}_{0}-\boldsymbol{I});\hskip 11.74988pt\boldsymbol{E}_{*}=\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{F}_{*}^{T}{\cdot}\boldsymbol{F}_{*}-\boldsymbol{I});\hskip 11.74988pt\boldsymbol{E}=\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{F}^{T}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}-\boldsymbol{I});\hskip 23.49976pt

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{E}_{0}=\boldsymbol{F}_{*}^{T}{\cdot}\boldsymbol{E}{\cdot}\boldsymbol{F}_{*}+\boldsymbol{E}_{*},

(4)

where the decomposition of
\boldsymbol{E}_{0}
in the last Eq. (
4
) can be checked using multiplicative decomposition in Eq. (
1
) and
\boldsymbol{I}
is the second-order unit tensor (see Appendix A). In the component form

\displaystyle E_{0,ij}=\frac{1}{2}(F_{0,ki}F_{0,kj}-\delta_{ij});\hskip 11.74988ptE_{*ij}=\frac{1}{2}(F_{*ki}F_{*kj}-\delta_{ij});\hskip 11.74988ptE_{ij}=\frac{1}{2}(F_{ki}F_{kj}-\delta_{ij});\hskip 23.49976pt

\displaystyle E_{0,ij}=F_{*ki}E_{kl}F_{*lj}+E_{*ij}.

(5)

2.2
Energy and stresses

We will not consider temperature or entropy variation here for brevity, but they can be trivially included.
Then the Helmholtz energy
\psi_{0}
for isothermal processes or the internal energy
U_{0}
for isentropic processes, per unit volume in the reference configuration
\Omega_{0}
and the intermediate configuration
\Omega_{*}
are

\begin{split}\psi_{0}=\psi_{0}(\boldsymbol{E}_{0});\hskip 23.49976pt\psi=\psi_{0}/J_{*}=\psi(0)+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}:\boldsymbol{E}+\tilde{\psi}(\boldsymbol{E}),\end{split}

(6)

where
\psi(0)
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}
are the energy and the second Piola-Kirchhoff stress at
\boldsymbol{E}=\mbox{\boldsymbol{$0$}}
(see Eq. (
8
)), and
\tilde{\psi}(\boldsymbol{E})
contains quadratic and higher order terms in
\boldsymbol{E}
. The constitutive equations and, in particular, elastic moduli, will be isothermal if the Helmholtz energy is used and isentropic (adiabatic) if the internal energy
U_{0}
is utilized. In the component form, Eq. (
6
) can be presented as

\hskip 23.49976pt\psi=\psi_{0}/J_{*}=\psi(0)+\sigma_{*ij}E_{ji}+\tilde{\psi}(E_{mn}).

(7)

The second Piola-Kirchhoff stress in the reference
\boldsymbol{T}_{0}
and intermediate
\boldsymbol{T}
configurations are defined as follows:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{T}_{0}}={J_{0}}\ {\boldsymbol{F}_{0}}^{-1}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot{\boldsymbol{F}_{0}}^{T-1}=\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}};\hskip 23.49976pt\boldsymbol{T}=J\boldsymbol{F}^{-1}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}^{T-1}=\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}+\frac{\partial\tilde{\psi}}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}};\hskip 11.74988pt

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{T}_{0}=J_{*}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{-1}_{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{T}\cdot{\boldsymbol{F}}^{T-1}_{*};\hskip 23.49976pt\boldsymbol{T}=J_{*}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{T}_{0}\cdot{\boldsymbol{F}}^{T}_{*};\hskip 23.49976pt\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}:=\boldsymbol{\sigma}|_{\boldsymbol{E}=0}=\boldsymbol{T}|_{\boldsymbol{E}=0}.

(8)

It is clear that for
\boldsymbol{F}=\boldsymbol{I}
one has
\boldsymbol{E}=\mbox{\boldsymbol{$0$}}
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\boldsymbol{T}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}
, which justifies presentation Eq. (
6
) for
\psi
. In the component form

\displaystyle T_{0,ij}={J_{0}}F_{0,ik}^{-1}\sigma_{kl}F_{0,jl}^{-1}=\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial E_{0,ij}};\hskip 23.49976ptT_{ij}={J}F_{ik}^{-1}\sigma_{kl}F_{jl}^{-1}=\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial E_{ij}}=\sigma_{*ij}+\frac{\partial\tilde{\psi}}{\partial E_{ij}};\hskip 11.74988pt

\displaystyle T_{0,ij}={J_{*}}F_{*ik}^{-1}T_{kl}F_{*jl}^{-1};\hskip 23.49976ptT_{ij}={J_{*}^{-1}}{F_{*ik}}T_{0,kl}{F_{*jl}}.

(9)

It follows from Eq. (
8
)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}={J_{0}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{F}_{0}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{T}_{0}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{F}_{0}}^{T}={J_{0}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{F}_{0}}\cdot\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{F}_{0}}^{T}={J}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{F}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{T}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{F}}^{T}={J}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{F}}\cdot\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{F}}^{T};

(10)

\displaystyle\sigma_{ij}=J_{0}^{-1}F_{0,ik}T_{0,kl}F_{0,jl}=J_{0}^{-1}F_{0,ik}\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial E_{0,kl}}F_{0,jl}=J^{-1}F_{ik}T_{kl}F_{jl}=J^{-1}F_{ik}\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial E_{kl}}F_{jl}.

(11)

Note that while
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}:=\boldsymbol{\sigma}|_{\boldsymbol{E}=0}=\boldsymbol{T}|_{\boldsymbol{E}=0}
has the physical meaning of stresses, in the thermodynamic treatment (e.g., application of thermodynamic laws to derive elasticity rules or temperature evolution equation),
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}
should be treated just as a constant in the linear term in Eq. (
7
) assuming the fixed intermediate configuration. The intermediate configuration and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}
can be varied in the final equations.

2.3
Elastic moduli in the reference and intermediate configurations

Elastic moduli in the reference
\boldsymbol{C}_{0}
and intermediate
\boldsymbol{C}
configurations are defined as follows:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{C}_{0}\left(\boldsymbol{E}_{0}\right):=\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{T}_{0}}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}}=\frac{\partial^{2}\psi_{0}}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}};\hskip 11.74988pt\boldsymbol{C}\left(\boldsymbol{E}\right):=\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{T}}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}}=\frac{\partial^{2}\psi}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}\partial\boldsymbol{E}};

(12)

\displaystyle C_{0,ijkl}\left(E_{0,mn}\right):=\frac{\partial T_{0,ij}}{\partial E_{0,kl}}=\frac{\partial^{2}\psi_{0}}{\partial E_{0,ij}\partial E_{0,kl}};\hskip 11.74988ptC_{ijkl}\left(E_{mn}\right):=\frac{\partial T_{ij}}{\partial E_{kl}}=\frac{\partial^{2}\psi}{\partial E_{ij}\partial E_{kl}}.

(13)

In particular,
\boldsymbol{C}\left(\boldsymbol{E}\right)
can be evaluated at
\boldsymbol{F}=\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}
, i.e., at
\boldsymbol{E}_{0}=\boldsymbol{E}_{*}
, when the intermediate configuration coincides with the current one, like in
[
30
,
31
]
.

To derive relationships between
\boldsymbol{C}
and
\boldsymbol{C}_{0}
, we first present

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{F}_{*}^{T}{\cdot}\boldsymbol{E}{\cdot}\boldsymbol{F}_{*}=\boldsymbol{F}_{*}^{T}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{*}}\boldsymbol{F}_{*}^{T}{\cdot}\boldsymbol{E}=\boldsymbol{F}_{*}^{T}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{*}}\boldsymbol{F}_{*}^{T}{\cdot}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}^{4}_{s}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{E};

(14)

\displaystyle F_{*il}^{T}E_{lk}F_{*kj}=F_{*li}F_{*kj}E_{lk}=\frac{1}{2}(F_{*li}F_{*kj}+F_{*ki}F_{*lj})E_{lk},

(15)

where we took into account symmetry of
E_{lk}
, or with the symmetrizing fourth-rank tensor,

\displaystyle F_{*li}F_{*kj}E_{lk}=F_{*li}F_{*kj}I_{s,lkmn}^{4}E_{nm}=\frac{1}{2}F_{*li}F_{*kj}(\delta_{ln}\delta_{km}+\delta_{lm}\delta_{kn})E_{nm}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(F_{*ni}F_{*mj}+F_{*mi}F_{*nj})E_{nm}=\frac{1}{2}(F_{*li}F_{*kj}+F_{*ki}F_{*lj})E_{lk}.

(16)

Then we evaluate

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}}=\frac{\partial(\boldsymbol{F}_{*}^{T}{\cdot}\boldsymbol{E}{\cdot}\boldsymbol{F}_{*})}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}}=\frac{\partial(\boldsymbol{F}_{*}^{T}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{*}}\boldsymbol{F}_{*}^{T}{\cdot}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}^{4}_{s}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{E})}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}}=\boldsymbol{F}_{*}^{T}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{*}}\boldsymbol{F}_{*}^{T}{\cdot}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}^{4}_{s};

(17)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial E_{0ij}}{\partial E_{kl}}=F_{*im}^{T}F_{*jn}^{T}I^{4}_{s,nmkl}=F_{*mi}F_{*nj}I^{4}_{s,nmkl}=\frac{1}{2}\left(F_{*ki}F_{*lj}+F_{*li}F_{*kj}\right),

(18)

which can be directly obtained from Eq. (
16
).
Note that in a similar expression in
[
30
,
31
]
, symmetrization is missing; this, however, did not affect the final results.

Next, using Eqs. (
8
) and (
17
), we obtain

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{T}=J_{*}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{*}\cdot\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{F}}^{T}_{*};\hskip 11.74988pt

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{C}=\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{T}}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}}=J_{*}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{*}}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{*}\cdot\frac{\partial^{2}\psi_{0}}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}\partial\boldsymbol{E}}=J_{*}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{*}}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{*}\cdot\frac{\partial^{2}\psi_{0}}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}}=

\displaystyle J_{*}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{*}}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{C}_{0}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\left(\boldsymbol{F}_{*}^{T}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{*}}\boldsymbol{F}_{*}^{T}{\cdot}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}^{4}_{s}\right)=\left(J_{*}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{*}}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{C}_{0}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}_{*}^{T}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{*}}\boldsymbol{F}_{*}^{T}\right)\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}^{4}_{s}=

\displaystyle J_{*}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{*}}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{C}_{0}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}_{*}^{T}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{*}}\boldsymbol{F}_{*}^{T}=J_{*}^{-1}\boldsymbol{F}_{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 4}}{{*}}\boldsymbol{F}_{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 3}}{{*}}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{*}}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{C}_{0}.

(19)

Similarly, in component form, we derive:

\displaystyle C_{ijkl}=\frac{\partial T_{ij}}{\partial E_{kl}}=J_{*}^{-1}{F}_{*in}{F}_{*jm}\frac{\partial^{2}\psi_{0}}{\partial E_{0,mn}\partial E_{kl}}=J_{*}^{-1}{F}_{*in}{F}_{*jm}\frac{\partial^{2}\psi_{0}}{\partial E_{0,mn}\partial E_{0,st}}\frac{\partial E_{0,st}}{\partial E_{kl}}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}J_{*}^{-1}{F}_{*in}{F}_{*jm}C_{0,mnst}\left(F_{*ks}F_{*lt}+F_{*ls}F_{*kt}\right)=J_{*}^{-1}F_{*lt}F_{*ks}{F}_{*in}{F}_{*jm}C_{0,mnst},

(20)

where we took into account symmetry of
C_{0,mnst}
, in the given case over permutation of
s
and
t
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{C}(\boldsymbol{E})=J_{*}^{-1}\boldsymbol{F}_{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 4}}{{*}}\boldsymbol{F}_{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 3}}{{*}}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{*}}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{C}_{0}(\boldsymbol{E}_{0});\hskip 11.74988ptC_{ijkl}(E_{ab})=J_{*}^{-1}{F}_{*im}{F}_{*jn}F_{*ks}F_{*lt}C_{0,mnst}(E_{0,ab}).

(21)

Eq. (
21
) gives the relationship between elastic moduli in two different configurations connected by deformation gradient
{\boldsymbol{F}}_{*}
. Note that
\boldsymbol{C}_{0}
is a function of
\boldsymbol{E}_{0}
and
\boldsymbol{C}
is a function of
\boldsymbol{E}
, and can be expressed via Eq. (
21
) in terms of
\boldsymbol{E}_{0}
and
\boldsymbol{F}_{*}
. They do not keep symmetry of the initial non-deformed lattice and for general
\boldsymbol{E}_{0}
they have symmetry of trigonal crystal, i.e., all 21 unequal components. They have complete Voigt symmetry, i.e., are invariant with respect to exchange indices
1\leftrightarrow 2
,
3\leftrightarrow 4
, and
(1,2)\leftrightarrow(3,4)
.

2.4
Elastic constants of all orders in the stress-free reference configuration

To find the functional relationship
\boldsymbol{C}_{0}\left(\boldsymbol{E}_{0}\right)
, we need to know the explicit expression for the energy
\psi_{0}(\boldsymbol{E}_{0})
. Usually, it is defined in terms of the elastic constants of all orders in the stress-free reference configuration.
Let us expand

\displaystyle\psi_{0}(\boldsymbol{E}_{0})=\psi_{0}(\mbox{\boldsymbol{$0$}})+1/2\boldsymbol{E}_{0}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{C}_{0}^{2}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{E}_{0}+1/(3!)(\boldsymbol{E}_{0}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{C}_{0}^{3}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{E}_{0})\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{E}_{0}+

\displaystyle 1/(4!)\boldsymbol{E}_{0}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}(\boldsymbol{E}_{0}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{C}_{0}^{4}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{E}_{0})\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{E}_{0}+1/(5!)(\boldsymbol{E}_{0}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}(\boldsymbol{E}_{0}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{C}_{0}^{4}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{E}_{0})\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{E}_{0})\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{E}_{0}+...\,,

(22)

\displaystyle\psi_{0}(E_{0,mn})=\psi_{0}(0)+1/2C_{0,ijkl}^{2}E_{0,ji}E_{0,lk}+1/(3!)C_{0,ijklef}^{3}E_{0,ij}E_{0,kl}E_{0,fe}+

\displaystyle 1/(4!)C_{0,ijklefqst}^{4}E_{0,ij}E_{0,kl}E_{0,fe}E_{0,ts}+1/(5!)C_{0,ijklefqstmn}^{5}E_{0,ij}E_{0,kl}E_{0,fe}E_{0,ts}E_{0,nm}+...\,,

(23)

where the
2n^{th}
-rank tensors
\boldsymbol{C}_{0}^{n}
are
n
-order elastic constants of the undeformed crystal, which
possess symmetry of the undeformed crystal, and contain complete information about elastic energy, moduli, and stress measures at any strain

\boldsymbol{E}_{0}
.

By definition,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{C}_{0}^{2}=:\frac{\partial^{2}\psi_{0}}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}}|_{\boldsymbol{E}_{0}=0}=\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{T}_{0}}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}}|_{\boldsymbol{E}_{0}=0};\hskip 23.49976pt\boldsymbol{C}_{0}^{3}=:\frac{\partial^{3}\psi_{0}}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}}|_{\boldsymbol{E}_{0}=0}=\frac{\partial^{2}\boldsymbol{T}_{0}}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}}|_{\boldsymbol{E}_{0}=0};

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{C}_{0}^{4}=:\frac{\partial^{4}\psi_{0}}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}}|_{\boldsymbol{E}_{0}=0}=\frac{\partial^{3}\boldsymbol{T}_{0}}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}}|_{\boldsymbol{E}_{0}=0};

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{C}_{0}^{5}=:\frac{\partial^{5}\psi_{0}}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}}|_{\boldsymbol{E}_{0}=0}=\frac{\partial^{4}\boldsymbol{T}_{0}}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}}|_{\boldsymbol{E}_{0}=0}.

(24)

\displaystyle C_{0,ijkl}^{2}=:\frac{\partial^{2}\psi_{0}}{\partial E_{0,ij}\partial E_{0,kl}}|_{E_{0,cd}=0}=\frac{\partial T_{0,ij}}{\partial E_{0,kl}}|_{E_{0,cd}=0};

(25)

\displaystyle C_{0,ijklef}^{3}:=\frac{\partial^{3}\psi_{0}}{\partial E_{0,ij}\partial E_{0,kl}\partial E_{0,ef}}|_{E_{0,cd}=0}=\frac{\partial^{2}T_{0,ij}}{\partial E_{0,kl}\partial E_{0,ef}}|_{E_{0,cd}=0};

\displaystyle C_{0,ijklefst}^{4}=:\frac{\partial^{4}\psi_{0}}{\partial E_{0,ij}\partial E_{0,kl}\partial E_{0,ef}\partial E_{0,st}}|_{E_{0,cd}=0}=\frac{\partial^{3}T_{0,ij}}{\partial E_{0,kl}\partial E_{0,ef}\partial E_{0,st}}|_{E_{0,cd}=0};

\displaystyle C_{0,ijklefstmn}^{5}=:\frac{\partial^{4}\psi_{0}}{\partial E_{0,ij}\partial E_{0,kl}\partial E_{0,ef}\partial E_{0,st}E_{0,mn}}|_{E_{0,cd}=0}=\frac{\partial^{3}T_{0,ij}}{\partial E_{0,kl}\partial E_{0,ef}\partial E_{0,st}\partial E_{0,mn}}|_{E_{0,cd}=0}.

Examples of elastic moduli are given up to third order in
[
56
,
57
,
58
]
, fourth order in
[
59
,
60
]
at small strains (e.g., 0.02-0.03), and up to fifth order for finite strains (0.37 for Si I) in
[
61
]
.

The knowledge of the above elastic constants allows one to determine elastic moduli
\boldsymbol{C}_{0}\left(\boldsymbol{E}_{0}\right)

according to Eq. (
12
),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{C}_{0}\left(\boldsymbol{E}_{0}\right)=\boldsymbol{C}_{0}^{2}+\boldsymbol{C}_{0}^{3}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{E}_{0}+1/2(\boldsymbol{C}_{0}^{4}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{E}_{0})\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{E}_{0}+1/(3!)((\boldsymbol{C}_{0}^{5}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{E}_{0})\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{E}_{0})\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{E}_{0}+...\,;

(26)

\displaystyle C_{0,ijkl}\left(E_{0,ab}\right)=C_{0,ijkl}^{2}+C_{0,ijklef}^{3}E_{0,fe}+1/2C_{0,ijklefst}^{4}E_{0,fe}E_{0,ts}+

\displaystyle 1/(3!)C_{0,ijklefstmn}^{5}E_{0,fe}E_{0,ts}E_{0,nm}+...\,.

(27)

Eq. (
21
) can be utilized to determine moduli
\boldsymbol{C}(\boldsymbol{E})
for an arbitrary intermediate configuration.
In particular, they can be evaluated at
\boldsymbol{F}=\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}
, i.e., at
\boldsymbol{E}_{0}=\boldsymbol{E}_{*}
, when the intermediate configuration coincides with the current one, like in
[
30
,
31
]
. For finite deviation of the intermediate configuration from the current one, one may need higher order elastic moduli in the intermediate configuration to evaluate energy and stresses.
That is why the choice of the intermediate configuration that coincides with the current one is the most convenient and popular.

3
RATE OF THE CAUCHY STRESS VERSUS DEFORMATION RATE

3.1
Kinematics

Let
\mbox{\boldsymbol{$v$}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}}({\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}}_{0},t)=\dot{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}}({\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}}_{0},t)
be the material velocity vector and
t
is time. Using the invertibility of the
relationship
{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}}={\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}}({\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}}_{0},t)
, i. e.,
{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}}_{0}={\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}}_{0}({\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}},t)
, velocity can be expressed as a function of
r
and
t
,
{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$v$}}}={\mbox{\boldsymbol{$v$}}}({\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}},t)
, i. e., in spatial (Eulerian) presentation.
Let us define the velocity gradients in configurations
\Omega_{0}
and
\Omega
, respectively:

\displaystyle{\partial\mbox{\boldsymbol{$v$}}\over\partial\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}_{0}}={\partial\over\partial\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}_{0}}{\partial\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}\over\partial t}={\partial\over\partial t}{\partial\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}\over\partial\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}_{0}}={\partial\over\partial t}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$F$}}=\dot{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$F$}}};

\displaystyle{\partial v_{i}\over\partial r_{0,j}}\,\mbox{\boldsymbol{$e$}}_{i}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$e$}}_{j}={\partial\over\partial r_{0,j}}{\partial r_{i}\over\partial t}\,\mbox{\boldsymbol{$e$}}_{i}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$e$}}_{j}={\partial\over\partial t}{\partial r_{i}\over\partial r_{0,j}}\,\mbox{\boldsymbol{$e$}}_{i}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$e$}}_{j}={\partial\over\partial t}F_{0,ij}\,\mbox{\boldsymbol{$e$}}_{i}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$e$}}_{j}=\dot{F}_{0,ij}\,\mbox{\boldsymbol{$e$}}_{i}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$e$}}_{j};

(28)

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldsymbol{$l$}}={\partial\mbox{\boldsymbol{$v$}}\over\partial\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}}={\partial\mbox{\boldsymbol{$v$}}\over\partial\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}_{0}}\cdot{\partial\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}_{0}\over\partial\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}}=\dot{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$F$}}}\cdot\mbox{\boldsymbol{$F$}}^{-1};\hskip 23.49976pt\mbox{\boldsymbol{$l$}}={\partial v_{i}\over\partial r_{j}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$e$}}_{i}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$e$}}_{j}={\partial v_{i}\over\partial r_{0,k}}\,{\partial r_{0,k}\over\partial r_{j}}\,\mbox{\boldsymbol{$e$}}_{i}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$e$}}_{j}=\dot{F}_{ik}F_{kj}^{-1}\,\mbox{\boldsymbol{$e$}}_{i}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$e$}}_{j}.

(29)

We took into account the permutability of the spatial and time derivative in the material description in terms of
({\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}}_{0},t)
.
As it follows from Eq. (
29
), this is not the case for variable reference configuration, i.e., in spatial presentation
in terms of
({\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}},t)
.

The velocity gradient can be decomposed into the symmetric deformation rate
\boldsymbol{d}
and the antisymmetric spin tensor

\boldsymbol{w}
:

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldsymbol{$l$}}=\boldsymbol{d}+\boldsymbol{w};\hskip 23.49976pt\boldsymbol{d}=\boldsymbol{l}_{s}=\left(\dot{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$F$}}}\cdot\mbox{\boldsymbol{$F$}}^{-1}\right)_{s};\hskip 23.49976pt\boldsymbol{w}=\boldsymbol{l}_{as}=\left(\dot{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$F$}}}\cdot\mbox{\boldsymbol{$F$}}^{-1}\right)_{as};

\displaystyle l_{ij}=d_{ij}+w_{ij};\hskip 23.49976ptd_{ij}=\left(\dot{F}_{ik}F_{kj}^{-1}\right)_{s};\hskip 23.49976ptw_{ij}=\left(\dot{F}_{ik}F_{kj}^{-1}\right)_{as}.

(30)

All these tensors are defined in the current configuration and independent of the reference configuration.
We will use known relationship between
\dot{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$E$}}}
and
d

[
63
,
52
]
:

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldsymbol{$d$}}=\mbox{\boldsymbol{$F$}}^{-1T}\cdot\dot{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$E$}}}\cdot\mbox{\boldsymbol{$F$}}^{-1};\hskip 23.49976pt\dot{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$E$}}}=\mbox{\boldsymbol{$F$}}^{T}\cdot\mbox{\boldsymbol{$d$}}\cdot\mbox{\boldsymbol{$F$}}.

(31)

3.2
Stress rate - deformation rate relationships

In computational mechanics, it is traditional to find the relationship between some objective time derivative of the Cauchy stress and deformation rate for different material models
[
62
]
. The coefficients in such relationships are used as an input in various computational codes (e.g., ABAQUS or DealII). We will do this here for nonlinear elasticity.
The time-derivative of the constitutive equation
\boldsymbol{T}=\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}}
for an arbitrarily chosen fixed intermediate configuration (including stress-free configuration) yields:

\displaystyle\dot{\boldsymbol{T}}=\boldsymbol{C}:\dot{\boldsymbol{E}};\hskip 23.49976pt\dot{T_{ij}}=C_{ijkl}\dot{E}_{lk}.

(32)

Note that because the intermediate configuration is fixed, there are no terms with
\dot{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{*}
and
\dot{\boldsymbol{F}}_{*}
.
It is derived in Appendix B that

\displaystyle\dot{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{d}:\boldsymbol{I})-\boldsymbol{l}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}-\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{l}^{T}=\frac{1}{J}(\boldsymbol{F}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{*}}\boldsymbol{F}\cdot\boldsymbol{C}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}^{T}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{*}}\boldsymbol{F}^{T}):\boldsymbol{d}=\bar{\boldsymbol{C}}:\boldsymbol{d},

(33)

where

\displaystyle\bar{\boldsymbol{C}}=\frac{1}{J}\boldsymbol{F}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{*}}\boldsymbol{F}\cdot\boldsymbol{C}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}^{T}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{*}}\boldsymbol{F}^{T}=\frac{1}{J}\boldsymbol{F}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 4}}{{*}}\boldsymbol{F}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 3}}{{*}}\boldsymbol{F}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{*}}\boldsymbol{F}\cdot\boldsymbol{C}=\frac{1}{J_{0}}\boldsymbol{F}_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 4}}{{*}}\boldsymbol{F}_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 3}}{{*}}\boldsymbol{F}_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{*}}\boldsymbol{F}_{0}\cdot\boldsymbol{C}_{0}.

(34)

Eq. (
21
) and multiplicative decomposition (
1
) were utilized.
The tensor
\bar{\boldsymbol{C}}
is the tensor of elastic moduli in the current configuration, which is independent of the choice of the reference configuration. Note that the intermediate configuration was utilized for generality; it is not necessary for the derivation of Eq. (
33
).

In component notations,

\displaystyle\dot{\sigma_{ij}}+\sigma_{ij}(d_{kl}\delta_{lk})-l_{il}\sigma_{lj}-\sigma_{im}l_{jm}=\frac{1}{J}F_{lt}F_{ks}{F}_{in}{F}_{jm}C_{mnst}d_{lk}=\bar{C}_{ijkl}d_{lk},

\displaystyle\bar{C}_{ijkl}=J^{-1}F_{lt}F_{ks}{F}_{in}{F}_{jm}C_{mnst}=J_{0}^{-1}F_{0,lt}F_{0,ks}{F}_{0,in}{F}_{0,jm}C_{0,mnst}.

(35)

The left-hand side (LHS) of Eq. (
33
) is known as the Truesdell objective rate of the Cauchy stress

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nabla}}{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{Tr}:=\dot{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{d}:\boldsymbol{I})-\boldsymbol{l}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}-\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{l}^{T}=\bar{\boldsymbol{C}}:\boldsymbol{d},

(36)

which gives one more physical interpretation of the tensor of elastic moduli in the current configuration
\bar{\boldsymbol{C}}
.
Using the definition of the Jaumann objective derivative
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nabla}}{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{J}:=\dot{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}-\boldsymbol{w}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}-\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}^{T}
in Eq. (
33
), it can be expressed as

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nabla}}{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{J}=\dot{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}-\boldsymbol{w}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}-\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}^{T}=\bar{\boldsymbol{C}}:\boldsymbol{d}-\boldsymbol{\sigma}\boldsymbol{I}:\boldsymbol{d}+\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{d};

(37)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nabla}}{{\sigma}}_{J,ij}=\dot{\sigma}_{ij}-w_{ik}\sigma_{kj}-\sigma_{ik}w_{jk}=\bar{C}_{ijkl}d_{lk}-\sigma_{ij}\delta_{mn}d_{nm}+d_{ik}\sigma_{kj}+\sigma_{ik}d_{kj}.

(38)

Using derivations in Appendix B, Eq. (
37
) can be transformed to

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nabla}}{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{J}=\dot{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}-\boldsymbol{w}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}-\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}^{T}=(\bar{\boldsymbol{C}}-\boldsymbol{\sigma}\boldsymbol{I}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{I}_{s}^{4}+(\boldsymbol{I}_{s}^{4}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma})^{T}):\boldsymbol{d}=\boldsymbol{B}:\boldsymbol{d},

(39)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{B}:=\bar{\boldsymbol{C}}-\boldsymbol{\sigma}\boldsymbol{I}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{I}_{s}^{4}+(\boldsymbol{I}_{s}^{4}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma})^{T};

(40)

\displaystyle B_{ijkl}:=\bar{C}_{ijkl}-\sigma_{ij}\delta_{kl}+\frac{1}{2}(\sigma_{il}\delta_{jk}+\sigma_{ik}\delta_{jl}+\sigma_{kj}\delta_{li}+\sigma_{lj}\delta_{ki}).

(41)

Thus, the relationship between the Jaumann derivative of the Cauchy stress and deformation rate is described by the effective elastic moduli tensor
\boldsymbol{B}
. The tensor
\boldsymbol{B}
was introduced in
[
29
]
as the coefficients in the relationship between stresses and small strains of the pre-deformed crystals and
in
[
30
,
31
]
as the derivative of the Cauchy stress with respect to small strain increment from the current configuration; thus, Eq. (
40
) is an alternative definition convenient for numerical realization in the modern finite-element codes (see Section
3.3
). It is easy to check directly (and since
\boldsymbol{B}
connects two symmetric tensors) that
B_{ijkl}
is symmetric with respect to permutations
i\leftrightarrow j
and
k\leftrightarrow l
, but is generally not symmetric in
(i,j)\leftrightarrow(k,l)
. The effective elastic moduli tensor
\boldsymbol{B}
is the main elastic moduli tensor used for the evaluation of crystal lattice instability

[
6
,
8
,
10
,
9
,
7
]
.
Here, it is introduced through the objective stress rate vs. deformation rate relationship.

Using transformations from Appendix B, Eq. (
39
) can also be presented as

\displaystyle\dot{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}=\boldsymbol{B}:\boldsymbol{d}+\left[(\boldsymbol{I}_{as}^{4}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma})^{T}-\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{I}_{as}^{4}\right]:\boldsymbol{w};

(42)

\displaystyle\dot{\sigma}_{ij}=B_{ijkl}d_{lk}+\frac{1}{2}(\sigma_{kj}\delta_{li}-\sigma_{jl}\delta_{ik}-\sigma_{il}\delta_{jk}+\sigma_{ik}\delta_{jl})w_{lk}.

(43)

3.3
Time integration

Let us introduce the distortion tensor
\boldsymbol{\beta}
with respect to the intermediate configuration
\Omega_{*}
, as well as its symmetric
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
and antisymmetric
\boldsymbol{\omega}
parts:

\boldsymbol{\beta}:=\boldsymbol{F}-\boldsymbol{I}=\boldsymbol{\epsilon}+\boldsymbol{\omega};\hskip 23.49976pt\boldsymbol{\epsilon}:=(\boldsymbol{\beta})_{s};\hskip 11.74988pt\boldsymbol{\omega}:=(\boldsymbol{\beta})_{as}.

(44)

For infinitesimal distortions, i.e., infinitesimal difference between the current and intermediate configurations,
|\boldsymbol{\beta}|\ll 1
, the velocity gradient is
\mbox{\boldsymbol{$l$}}\simeq\dot{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
, the deformation rate is

\boldsymbol{d}\simeq\dot{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}
, and spin is
\boldsymbol{w}\simeq\dot{\boldsymbol{\omega}}
. Then Eq. (
43
)
can be integrated for small time increment:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(t+\Delta t)=\boldsymbol{\sigma}(t)+\boldsymbol{B}:\dot{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}\Delta t+\left[(\boldsymbol{I}_{as}^{4}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma})^{T}-\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{I}_{as}^{4}\right]:\dot{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\Delta t;

(45)

\displaystyle{\sigma}_{ij}(t+\Delta t)={\sigma}_{ij}(t)+B_{ijkl}\dot{\epsilon}_{lk}\Delta t+\frac{1}{2}(\sigma_{kj}\delta_{li}-\sigma_{jl}\delta_{ik}-\sigma_{il}\delta_{jk}+\sigma_{ik}\delta_{jl})\dot{\omega}_{lk}\Delta t.

(46)

Eq. (
46
) is used in numerical algorithms for solution of boundary-value problems. Many finite-element programs (e.g., ABAQUS or DealII) use the fourth-rank tensor relating objective stress rate and deformation rate as the input from the user-developed material models.
While we presented the simplest explicit integration, any implicit integration scheme (e.g., predictor-corrector, backward Euler, Crank-Nicolson, etc.) can be applied in the traditional way.

If an intermediate configuration
\Omega_{*}
is updated, then small strain
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
and rotations
\boldsymbol{\omega}
are evaluated with respect to updated configuration
\Omega_{*}
without remembering previous values of
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
and
\boldsymbol{\omega}
, Eq. (
45
)
can be presented in terms of small strains
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}=\Delta\boldsymbol{\epsilon}=\dot{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}\Delta t
and rotations

\boldsymbol{\omega}=\Delta\boldsymbol{\omega}=\dot{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\Delta t

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(t+\Delta t)=\boldsymbol{\sigma}(t)+\boldsymbol{B}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon}+\left[(\boldsymbol{I}_{as}^{4}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma})^{T}-\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{I}_{as}^{4}\right]:\boldsymbol{\omega},

(47)

\displaystyle{\sigma}_{ij}(t+\Delta t)={\sigma}_{ij}(t)+B_{ijkl}\epsilon_{lk}+\frac{1}{2}(\sigma_{kj}\delta_{li}-\sigma_{jl}\delta_{ik}-\sigma_{il}\delta_{jk}+\sigma_{ik}\delta_{jl})\omega_{lk}.

(48)

4
BULK MODULUS AND COMPRESSIBILITY

While it is well known that the bulk modulus
K:=-V\frac{\partial p}{\partial V}
, where
p=-{1\over 3}\boldsymbol{\sigma}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}
is the pressure and

V
is the deformed volume of the system, we need to understand how this follows from definitions used above and which elastic moduli does
K
correspond to:
\boldsymbol{C}_{0}
,
\bar{\boldsymbol{C}}
, or
B
? For this purpose, we need to give strict definitions of the bulk modulus and compressibility for the general stress-strain state.
Also, the importance of mentioning the exact components of the strain or stress tensors that are fixed in the chosen definition of the bulk moduli and compressibility for the general stress-strain state is illustrated.

4.1
Hydrostatic loading of material with elastic energy depending on volume

For liquids and gases, since under hydrostatic loading stress work per unit reference volume
V_{0}
or more generally,
V_{*}
(in the intermediate configuration) is
-V_{*}^{-1}pdV=-pdJ
, it then follows from

-V_{*}^{-1}pdV=-pdJ=d\psi\hskip 11.74988pt\rightarrow\hskip 11.74988ptp=-\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial J}=-{V_{*}}\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial V},

(49)

where we remind that
\psi
is the elastic energy per unit volume
V_{*}
in the intermediate configuration
\Omega_{*}
.
Then the bulk modulus in the intermediate configuration
{K}_{*}
can be defined as

{K}_{*}:=-\frac{\partial p}{\partial J}=\frac{\partial^{2}{\psi}}{\partial{J}^{2}}=-V_{*}\frac{\partial p}{\partial V}=V_{*}^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}{\psi}}{\partial V^{2}}.

(50)

We use subscript
*
to distinguish this modulus from the moduli
\boldsymbol{C}
in the intermediate configuration because for
{K}_{*}
the derivative is calculated for the Cauchy stress, while for
\boldsymbol{C}
it was evaluated for the second Piola-Kirchhoff stress.
When the intermediate configuration coincides with the current one,
then
V_{*}=V
, elastic energy is defined per unit current volume and is designated as
\psi_{c}
, and

p=-{V}\frac{\partial\psi_{c}}{\partial V};\hskip 23.49976pt{K}=-V\frac{\partial p}{\partial V}=V^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}{\psi_{c}}}{\partial V^{2}}=-\frac{\partial p}{\partial lnV},

(51)

i.e., the classical definition is obtained. The following alternative expressions for
K
are valid for any

fixed
intermediate configuration:

{K}=-\frac{V}{V_{*}}\frac{\partial p}{\partial(V/V_{*})}=-J\frac{\partial p}{\partial J}=J\frac{\partial^{2}\psi}{\partial J^{2}}=-\frac{\partial p}{\partial\varepsilon_{0}}=JK_{*};\hskip 23.49976pt\varepsilon_{0}:={\ln J},

(52)

where
\varepsilon_{0}
is the logarithmic volumetric strain.
Comparisons of Eqs. (
51
) and (
52
) leads to
dlnV=d\varepsilon_{0}=d\ln\frac{V}{V_{0}}=\frac{dV}{V_{0}}\frac{V_{0}}{V}
, which means that the increment of the logarithmic strain is independent of the reference configuration (volume).

Compressibility or bulk compliance is determined as

k=\frac{1}{K}=-\frac{1}{V}\frac{\partial V}{\partial p}=-\frac{\partial lnV}{\partial p}=-\frac{\partial\varepsilon_{0}}{\partial p}=-\frac{1}{J}\frac{\partial J}{\partial p}.

(53)

Note that all the above equations are strict if
\varepsilon_{0}
is the only strain-related variable; otherwise, derivatives should be calculated
at fixed other strain-related variables or deviatoric stress, which will be discussed below.

4.2
General elastic material

Bulk modulus.
Let us consider general stress-strain state and find explicit connection between
K
and
\boldsymbol{B}
. We decompose the Cauchy stress and the deformation rate into spherical and deviatoric (
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{dev}
and
\boldsymbol{d}_{dev}
) parts

\boldsymbol{\sigma}=-p\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{dev};\hskip 23.49976pt\boldsymbol{d}=\frac{1}{3}\dot{\varepsilon}_{0}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}+\boldsymbol{d}_{dev};\hskip 11.74988pt\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{dev}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}=\boldsymbol{d}_{dev}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}=\mbox{\boldsymbol{$0$}},

(54)

since
\boldsymbol{d}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}=\dot{J}/J=\dot{\ln J}=\dot{\varepsilon}_{0}

[
63
,
52
]
.
Substituting
Eq. (
54
) in Eq. (
39
), we obtain

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nabla}}{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{J}=-\dot{p}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}+\dot{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{dev}-\boldsymbol{w}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{dev}-\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{dev}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}^{T}=\boldsymbol{B}:({1}/{3}\,\dot{\varepsilon}_{0}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}+\boldsymbol{d}_{dev}),

(55)

where we took into account that
\boldsymbol{w}\cdot\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}+\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}^{T}=\mbox{\boldsymbol{$0$}}
. Calculating trace of Eq. (
55
),
we derive

\displaystyle-\dot{p}={1}/{9}\,\dot{\varepsilon}_{0}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{B}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}+{1}/{3}\,\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{B}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{d}_{dev}={1}/{9}\,\dot{\varepsilon}_{0}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{B}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}+{1}/{3}\,\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{B}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\dot{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{dev}.

(56)

One can define different bulk moduli using Eq. (
52
), i.e.,
-\frac{\partial p}{\partial\varepsilon_{0}}
, under various constraints on the strain or stress states. One of the natural definitions is to fix deviatoric strain; then from Eq. (
56
) we obtain:

\displaystyle K_{V}=-\frac{\partial p}{\partial\varepsilon_{0}}|_{{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{dev}}=1/9\,\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{B}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}=1/9\,B_{iijj}=1/9\,(\bar{C}_{iijj}+3p).

(57)

Subscript
V
is for Voigt, because relationship (
57
) between
K_{V}
and
\boldsymbol{B}
corresponds to the bulk modulus of the polycrystalline aggregate based on the Voigt averaging (i.e., for the same strain in all crystals
[
69
,
70
,
65
]
). The last equality was obtained using relationship (
41
) between the tensors
\boldsymbol{B}
and
\bar{\boldsymbol{C}}
for an arbitrary
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
.
Thus,
K_{V}
corresponds to the moduli
B
, which is clear since it connects components of the Jaumann rate of the Cauchy stress and the deformation rate.

Note that generally, the bulk modulus in Eq. (
57
) can be determined for arbitrary strain or stress states (which contribute to the definition of
\boldsymbol{B}
); the small strain increment should be pure volumetric (isotropic) only. In particular, the current state can be an isotropically
strained material with respect to stress-free state or material under hydrostatic pressure
p
, which produces a generally anisotropic strain.
In particular, let one impose in atomistic simulations
\boldsymbol{F}=J^{1/3}\boldsymbol{I}
and calculate energy
\psi(J)
.
Then from the power balance

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}:\boldsymbol{d}dt=-pd{\varepsilon}_{0}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{dev}:\boldsymbol{d}_{dev}dt=J^{-1}d\psi,

(58)

where we took into account Eq. (
54
),
\boldsymbol{d}_{dev}=\mbox{\boldsymbol{$0$}}
implies

\displaystyle-pd{\varepsilon}_{0}=-pJ^{-1}dJ=J^{-1}d\psi(J)\hskip 11.74988pt\rightarrow\hskip 11.74988ptp=-\frac{\partial\psi(J)}{\partial J}|_{\boldsymbol{F}=J^{1/3}\boldsymbol{I}}.

(59)

Then Eqs. (
57
) and (
59
) imply
that the same relationships like in Section
4.1
are applicable:

\displaystyle{K}_{V}=-\frac{\partial p}{\partial\varepsilon_{0}}|_{\boldsymbol{F}=J^{1/3}\boldsymbol{I}}=-J\frac{\partial p}{\partial J}|_{\boldsymbol{F}=J^{1/3}\boldsymbol{I}}=J\frac{\partial^{2}\psi}{\partial J^{2}}|_{\boldsymbol{F}=J^{1/3}\boldsymbol{I}}=

\displaystyle-V\frac{\partial p}{\partial V}|_{\boldsymbol{F}=J^{1/3}\boldsymbol{I}}=V^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}{\psi_{c}}}{\partial V^{2}}|_{\boldsymbol{F}=J^{1/3}\boldsymbol{I}}.

(60)

Compressibility.

Let us introduce the fourth-rank compliance tensor
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
, inverse to
\boldsymbol{B}
, by relationship
\boldsymbol{\lambda}:\boldsymbol{B}=\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}^{4}_{s}
(
\lambda_{ijmn}B_{nmlk}=\frac{1}{2}(\delta_{il}\delta_{jk}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl})
). Then producing double contraction of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
and both sides of Eq. (
55
), we obtain

\displaystyle{1}/{3}\,\dot{\varepsilon}_{0}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}+\boldsymbol{d}_{dev}=\boldsymbol{\lambda}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\left(-\dot{p}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}+\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nabla}}{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{dev,J}\right).

(61)

Trace of Eq. (
61
) has the form

\displaystyle\dot{\varepsilon}_{0}=-\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{\lambda}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}\dot{p}+\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{\lambda}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nabla}}{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{dev,J}.

(62)

Similar to the bulk moduli, one can define different compressibilities using Eq. (
53
), i.e.,

-\frac{\partial\varepsilon_{0}}{\partial p}
, under various constraints on the strain or stress states. One of the natural definitions is to impose
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nabla}}{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{dev,J}=\mbox{\boldsymbol{$0$}}
, i.e., to fix deviatoric stress
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{dev}
and rotations
\boldsymbol{\omega}
; then from Eq. (
62
) we obtain the bulk compliance (compressibility):

\displaystyle k_{R}=-\frac{\partial\varepsilon_{0}}{\partial p}|_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{dev}}=\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{\lambda}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}=\lambda_{iijj}.

(63)

Here subscript
R
is for Reuss because relationship (
63
) between
k_{R}
and
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
corresponds to the compressibility of the polycrystalline aggregate based on the Reuss averaging (i.e., for the same stress in all crystals)
[
64
,
65
]
; subscript
\boldsymbol{\omega}
for fixed rotation is omitted for brevity but should always be kept in mind.
Similar to the bulk modulus, generally, the compressibility in Eq. (
63
) can be determined for arbitrary strain or stress states (which contribute to the definition of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
); the small stress increment should be pure hydrostatic only. In particular, the current state can be an isotropically
strained material with respect to the stress-free state or material under hydrostatic pressure
p
, which generally produces anisotropic strain.

Note that generally

K_{V}\neq{1}/{k_{R}}
,
because derivatives
\frac{\partial p}{\partial\varepsilon_{0}}|_{{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{dev}}
and
\frac{\partial\varepsilon_{0}}{\partial p}|_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{dev}}
are evaluated while fixing different parameters that do not correspond to each other. It is known that
K_{V}=1/k_{R}
for cubic crystals and isotropic materials
only, because for them fixation of small deviatoric strain and stress is equivalent.

One can define alternative bulk modulus
K_{R}
and compressibility
k_{V}
by equations

\displaystyle K_{R}:=-\frac{\partial p}{\partial\varepsilon_{0}}|_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{dev}}=\frac{1}{k_{R}}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{iijj}};\hskip 23.49976ptk_{V}:=-\frac{\partial\varepsilon_{0}}{\partial p}|_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{dev}}=\frac{1}{K_{V}}=\frac{9}{B_{iijj}}.

(64)

In particular, if for the entire straining
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{dev}=\mbox{\boldsymbol{$0$}}
and loading is hydrostatic, then
energy can be calculated for hydrostatic loading as
\psi(J)
, and
Eq. (
59
) implies

\displaystyle-pJ^{-1}dJ=J^{-1}d\psi(J)\hskip 11.74988pt\rightarrow\hskip 11.74988ptp=-\frac{\partial\psi(J)}{\partial J}|_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{dev}=0}.

(65)

Then again, the same relationships like in Section
4.1
are applicable:

\displaystyle{K}_{R}=-\frac{\partial p}{\partial\varepsilon_{0}}|_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{dev}=0}=-J\frac{\partial p}{\partial J}|_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{dev}=0}=J\frac{\partial^{2}\psi}{\partial J^{2}}|_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{dev}=0}=-V\frac{\partial p}{\partial V}|_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{dev}=0}=V^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}{\psi_{c}}}{\partial V^{2}}|_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{dev}=0}.

(66)

To summarize, there is no unique bulk modulus and compressibility under general stress-strain states; they can be defined by derivatives

-\frac{\partial p}{\partial\varepsilon_{0}}
and
-\frac{\partial\varepsilon_{0}}{\partial p}
, respectively, under various constraints on the strain or stress states, which should be very clearly stated. For one of them,
K_{V}=1/k_{V}
, we fix deviatoric strain, and for another,
K_{R}=1/k_{R}
, we fix deviatoric stress. Generally, the bulk modulus and compressibility can be determined for arbitrary strain or stress states (which contribute to the definition of
\boldsymbol{B}
). For
K_{V}=1/k_{V}
small strain increment should be pure volumetric (isotropic) only and
for
K_{R}=1/k_{R}
small stress increment should be pure hydrostatic pressure only. If the entire loading corresponds to isotropic straining or hydrostatic loading, then two different energy functions
\psi(J)
can be defined, and bulk modulus for both loadings can be in addition defined in terms of the second derivative of energy. While there is no averaging in the definition of the bulk modulus and compressibility for a single crystal, the expression for
K_{V}=1/k_{V}
in terms of components
B_{ij}
coincides with the Voigt average for polycrystal, and expression for
k_{R}=1/K_{R}
in terms of components
\lambda_{ij}
coincides with the Reuss average. Neglecting the difference between the two types of bulk moduli/compressibilities may lead to quantitative misinterpretation of experiments and qualitative contradictions, which will be illustrated in Section
6.2.3
.

5
APPROXIMATION FOR SMALL DISTORTIONS WITH RESPECT TO AN INTERMEDIATE CONFIGURATION

5.1
Elastic energy

Using Eqs. (
4
) and (
44
), for the general case of finite distortions
\boldsymbol{\beta}=\boldsymbol{F}-\boldsymbol{I}=\boldsymbol{\epsilon}+\boldsymbol{\omega}

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{E}=\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{F}^{T}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}-\boldsymbol{I})=\boldsymbol{\epsilon}+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{T}\cdot\boldsymbol{\beta}=\boldsymbol{\epsilon}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{\epsilon}+2(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{\omega})_{s}+\boldsymbol{\omega}^{T}\cdot\boldsymbol{\omega}\right).\hskip 23.49976pt

(67)

The energy per unit volume in the intermediate configuration
\Omega_{*}
in the quadratic in
\boldsymbol{E}
approximation is

\begin{split}\psi=\psi_{0}/J_{*}=\psi(0)+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}:\boldsymbol{E}+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{E}:\boldsymbol{C}:\boldsymbol{E}.\,\end{split}

(68)

The corresponding second Piola-Kirchhoff stress is

\boldsymbol{T}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}+\boldsymbol{C}:\boldsymbol{E}.

(69)

For small distortions
\boldsymbol{\beta}
, substituting Eq. (
67
)
in Eq. (
68
), we obtain in quadratic in distortions approximation

\displaystyle\psi

\displaystyle=\psi(0)+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}:\left(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{T}\cdot\boldsymbol{\beta}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{T}:\boldsymbol{C}:\boldsymbol{\beta}^{T};

(70)

\displaystyle\psi

\displaystyle=\psi(0)+\sigma_{*ij}\left(\epsilon_{ji}+\frac{1}{2}\beta_{kj}\beta_{ki}\right)+\frac{1}{2}{C}_{ijkl}\beta_{ij}\beta_{kl},

(71)

where
\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{T}:\boldsymbol{C}:\boldsymbol{\beta}^{T}=\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}:\boldsymbol{C}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
due to symmetry of
\boldsymbol{C}
, but we will keep the expression with
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{T}
for a while in order to express the final quadratic form like
\frac{1}{2}\tilde{C}_{ijkl}\beta_{ij}\beta_{kl}
with moduli
\tilde{C}_{ijkl}
to be determined.

The second Piola-Kirchhoff stress in linear in distortions approximation reduces to

\boldsymbol{T}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}+\boldsymbol{C}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon}.

(72)

We transform

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}:\boldsymbol{\beta}^{T}\cdot\boldsymbol{\beta}=\boldsymbol{\beta}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}:\boldsymbol{\beta}^{T}=\boldsymbol{\beta}^{T}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\left(\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}^{4}_{t}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}\right):\boldsymbol{\beta}^{T};

(73)

\displaystyle\beta_{ij}I^{4}_{t,ijkl}\sigma_{*lm}\beta_{km}=\sigma_{*lm}\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}\beta_{ij}\beta_{km}=\sigma_{*jm}\delta_{ik}\beta_{ij}\beta_{km}=\sigma_{*jl}\delta_{ik}\beta_{ij}\beta_{kl}.

(74)

or more straightforwardly

\sigma_{*ij}\beta_{kj}\beta_{ki}=\sigma_{*ij}\delta_{mk}\beta_{kj}\beta_{mi}=\sigma_{*lj}\delta_{ki}\beta_{ij}\beta_{kl}.

Then,

\displaystyle\psi=\psi(0)+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon}+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{T}:\tilde{\boldsymbol{C}}:\boldsymbol{\beta}^{T}=\psi(0)+\sigma_{*ij}\epsilon_{ji}+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{C}_{ijkl}\beta_{ij}\beta_{kl};

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{C}}:=\boldsymbol{C}+\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}^{4}_{t}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*};\hskip 23.49976pt\tilde{C}_{ijkl}:=C_{ijkl}+\sigma_{*lj}\delta_{ki}.

(75)

The tensor
\tilde{C}_{ijkl}
is not symmetric with respect to
i\leftrightarrow j
and
k\leftrightarrow l
and does not possess the Voigt symmetry, but it is
symmetric in exchange between pairs
ij
and
kl
.
Eq. (
75
) was suggested in
[
27
]
. For the intermediate configuration infinitesimally close to the current one, we have
\bar{\boldsymbol{C}}\simeq\boldsymbol{C}
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}\simeq\boldsymbol{\sigma}
. Then it follows from the comparison of Eqs. (
40
) and (
75
) that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{B}:=\tilde{\boldsymbol{C}}-\boldsymbol{I}^{4}_{t}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}-\boldsymbol{\sigma}\boldsymbol{I}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{I}_{s}^{4}+(\boldsymbol{I}_{s}^{4}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma})^{T}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{C}}-\boldsymbol{\sigma}\boldsymbol{I}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{I}_{s}^{4}+(\boldsymbol{I}_{as}^{4}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma})^{T};

(76)

\displaystyle B_{ijkl}:=\tilde{C}_{ijkl}-\sigma_{ij}\delta_{kl}+\frac{1}{2}(\sigma_{il}\delta_{jk}+\sigma_{ik}\delta_{jl}+\sigma_{kj}\delta_{li}-\sigma_{lj}\delta_{ki}).

(77)

Note that if higher-order terms in
\boldsymbol{E}
will be utilized in Eq. (
68
), stress
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}
will not contribute to the higher-order terms in
\boldsymbol{\beta}
, because
\boldsymbol{E}
does not contain higher than the second-order terms in
\boldsymbol{\beta}
.
Decomposing
\boldsymbol{\beta}
into
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
and
\boldsymbol{\omega}
in Eq. (
67
), we obtain (see Appendix B)

\displaystyle\psi=\psi(0)+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon}+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}:{\boldsymbol{C}}^{\epsilon\epsilon}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon}+\boldsymbol{\omega}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}{\boldsymbol{C}}^{\boldsymbol{\omega}\epsilon}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\omega}:{\boldsymbol{C}}^{\boldsymbol{\omega}\boldsymbol{\omega}}:\boldsymbol{\omega}^{T};

(78)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{C}^{\epsilon\epsilon}:={\boldsymbol{C}}+\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}^{4}_{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}:\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}^{4}_{s};\hskip 23.49976pt{\boldsymbol{C}}^{\boldsymbol{\omega}\epsilon}:=\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}^{4}_{as}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}:\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}^{4}_{s};\hskip 23.49976pt{\boldsymbol{C}}^{\boldsymbol{\omega}\boldsymbol{\omega}}:=\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}^{4}_{as}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}:\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}^{4}_{as};

\displaystyle\psi=\psi(0)+\sigma_{*ij}\epsilon_{ji}+\frac{1}{2}C^{\epsilon\epsilon}_{ijkl}\epsilon_{ij}\epsilon_{kl}+C^{\omega\epsilon}_{ijkl}\epsilon_{kl}\omega_{ji}+\frac{1}{2}C^{\omega\omega}_{ijkl}\omega_{kl}\omega_{ji};

\displaystyle C^{\epsilon\epsilon}_{ijkl}:=C_{ijkl}+\frac{1}{4}(\sigma_{*lj}\delta_{ki}+\sigma_{*li}\delta_{kj}+\sigma_{*jk}\delta_{li}+\sigma_{*ik}\delta_{lj});

\displaystyle C^{\omega\epsilon}_{ijkl}:=\frac{1}{4}(\sigma_{*li}\delta_{kj}-\sigma_{*lj}\delta_{ki}-\sigma_{*jk}\delta_{li}+\sigma_{*ik}\delta_{lj});

\displaystyle C^{\omega\omega}_{ijkl}:=\frac{1}{4}(\sigma_{*li}\delta_{kj}-\sigma_{*lj}\delta_{ki}+\sigma_{*jk}\delta_{li}-\sigma_{*ik}\delta_{lj}).

(79)

The tensor
{\boldsymbol{C}}^{\epsilon\epsilon}
possesses full Voigt symmetry. The tensor
C^{\omega\epsilon}_{ijkl}
is symmetric in
k\leftrightarrow l
and antisymmetric in
i\leftrightarrow j
. The tensor
C^{\omega\omega}_{ijkl}
is antisymmetric in
i\leftrightarrow j
and
k\leftrightarrow l
, and invariant under exchange of pairs
(ij)\leftrightarrow(lk)
,

(kl)\leftrightarrow(ji)
,
(ij)\leftrightarrow(kl)
.

A delicate moment in consistent keeping in Eq. (
70
) all quadratic in distortions terms is that in the linear in
\boldsymbol{E}
term, we keep all quadratic terms, but in quadratic in
\boldsymbol{E}
term, we retain the linear term
\boldsymbol{E}=\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
only. This modifies elastic moduli
\boldsymbol{C}
by
\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}^{4}_{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}:\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}^{4}_{s}
and also shows that the energy depends on the small rotations
\boldsymbol{\omega}
. Small rotations may be related to
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
for specific loadings (e.g., for simple shear), thus also changing elastic moduli.

As a general conclusion, it follows from Eqs. (
72
), (
47
),
and (
78
) that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{T}\neq\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}};\hskip 23.49976pt\boldsymbol{C}\neq\frac{\partial^{2}\psi}{\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}};\hskip 23.49976pt\boldsymbol{\sigma}\neq\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}};\hskip 23.49976pt\boldsymbol{\sigma}\neq\frac{1}{J}\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}};\hskip 23.49976pt\boldsymbol{B}\neq\frac{\partial^{2}\psi}{\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}},

(80)

despite that this is expected from small strain theory.

In many works (e.g.,
[
44
,
45
,
46
]
), where the effect of initial stresses and rotations is neglected, the energy under the small strain increment in
\Omega_{*}
is approximated like in traditional small strain theory, i.e.

\displaystyle\psi-\psi(0)\simeq\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{C}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}.

(81)

In atomistic simulations, the energy is calculated for different distortions
\boldsymbol{\beta}
and then is approximated by quadratic function Eq. (
81
). If these evaluations are performed under initial stresses and with small rotations, then all neglected terms in Eq. (
78
) are attributed to
\boldsymbol{C}
and produce significant error in
\boldsymbol{C}
. This will be shown in the examples below.

5.2
Equation of motion and wave propagation in the pre-stressed solids

It is shown, e.g., in
[
27
]
that the Lagrangian equations of motion is

\displaystyle\rho{\frac{\partial^{2}u_{i}}{\partial t^{2}}}=\tilde{C}_{ijkl}^{s}\frac{\partial^{2}u_{k}}{\partial r_{*j}\partial r_{*l}};\hskip 23.49976pt\tilde{C}_{ijkl}^{s}:=\frac{1}{2}(\tilde{C}_{ijkl}+\tilde{C}_{ilkj}),

(82)

i. e., it utilizes the same tensor
\tilde{\boldsymbol{C}}
like in Eq. (
75
) for the energy but symmetrized with respect to
j
and
l
, because the second derivative in Eq. (
82
) is symmetric with respect to
j
and
l
. Here
u_{i}
are components of the displacement vector
\mbox{\boldsymbol{$u$}}=\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}-\mbox{\boldsymbol{$r$}}_{*}
. A similar equation of motion was derived in

[
30
]
in a different way. While
[
27
]
gave a pure mechanical interpretation of
\tilde{\boldsymbol{C}}
,
[
30
]
has generalized it for thermoelasticity.

Eq. (
82
) results in the following plane wave propagation equation
[
27
,
30
]

\displaystyle\rho v_{w}^{2}u_{i}=\tilde{C}_{ijkl}^{s}k_{j}k_{l}u_{k}=L_{ik}u_{k};\hskip 23.49976ptL_{ik}:=\tilde{C}_{ijkl}^{s}k_{j}k_{l},

(83)

where
v_{w}
is the wave velocity,
k_{i}
are the components of the unit vector in the direction of wave propagation, and
L_{ik}
is the propagation matrix. As one of the known stability conditions, the propagation matrix
L_{ik}
should be a positive definite for all possible
k_{i}
to guarantee possibility of wave propagation in any direction, which shows an additional importance of the tensor
\tilde{\boldsymbol{C}}
. Eq. (
83
) shows the possibility to determine
the tensor
\tilde{\boldsymbol{C}}^{s}
from the wave propagation experiment, while the antisymmetric in
j
and
l
part of
\tilde{C}_{ijkl}

can be determined from the expression for elastic energy only.

5.3
Simple shear

Figure 2:
Simple shear
\boldsymbol{F}=\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}+\gamma\boldsymbol{m}\boldsymbol{n}
with the shear strain
\gamma
at the plane with the unit normal
\boldsymbol{n}
along the unit direction
\boldsymbol{m}
. Crystal lattice of any symmetry can be oriented arbitrarily.

Let us consider a simple shear as an example:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{F}-\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}=\boldsymbol{\beta}=\gamma\boldsymbol{m}\boldsymbol{n}=\gamma\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0\\
0&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
\end{pmatrix};

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{E}=\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{F}^{T}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}-\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}})=\frac{1}{2}\gamma(\boldsymbol{m}\boldsymbol{n}+\boldsymbol{n}\boldsymbol{m}+\gamma\boldsymbol{n}\boldsymbol{n})=\frac{1}{2}\gamma\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0\\
1&\gamma&0\\
0&0&0\\
\end{pmatrix}.

(84)

Here,
\boldsymbol{n}
is the unit normal to the shear plane,
\boldsymbol{m}
is the unit vector in the shear direction,
\gamma
is the shear strain,
and the matrices are presented in the coordinate system shown in Fig.
2
.
Combining all terms from Appendix B, we obtain from Eq. (
79
) for the energy

\displaystyle\psi=\psi(0)+\sigma_{*12}\epsilon_{12}+\frac{1}{2}\underbrace{(C_{1212}+\frac{1}{4}(\sigma_{*11}+\sigma_{*22}))}_{C_{1212}^{\epsilon\epsilon}}\gamma^{2}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{4}(\sigma_{*22}-\sigma_{*11})}_{C^{\omega\epsilon}_{2121}}\gamma^{2}+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{2}\underbrace{\frac{1}{4}(\sigma_{*22}+\sigma_{*11})}_{C^{\omega\omega}_{1221}}=\psi(0)+\sigma_{*12}\epsilon_{12}+\frac{1}{2}\underbrace{(C_{1212}+\sigma_{*22})}_{C_{\psi}}\gamma^{2}.

(85)

The last equation in Eq. (
85
) can be obtained more quickly by substituting
\boldsymbol{\beta}
from Eq. (
84
) in Eq. (
70
).
Note that from Eq. (
41
)

\displaystyle B_{1212}={C}_{1212}+\frac{1}{2}(\sigma_{*11}+\sigma_{*22})=C_{1212}^{\epsilon\epsilon}+C^{\omega\omega}_{1221}\neq C_{1212}^{\epsilon\epsilon}\neq C_{\psi}.

(86)

For the straining with
\boldsymbol{\beta}=\boldsymbol{\epsilon}=\frac{1}{2}\gamma(\boldsymbol{m}\boldsymbol{n}+\boldsymbol{n}\boldsymbol{m})
and
\boldsymbol{\omega}=\mbox{\boldsymbol{$0$}}
(rotation-free shear), the terms with
C^{\omega\epsilon}_{1212}
and
C^{\omega\omega}_{1212}
disappear from the expression for
\psi
and one obtains

\displaystyle\psi=\psi(0)+\sigma_{*12}\epsilon_{12}+\frac{1}{2}\underbrace{(C_{1212}+\frac{1}{4}(\sigma_{*11}+\sigma_{*22}))}_{C_{1212}^{\epsilon\epsilon}}\gamma^{2}.

(87)

Again,
B_{1212}\neq C_{1212}^{\epsilon\epsilon}
.

The above example explicitly shows the effect of initial stress and small rotations on the energy and determination of the elastic moduli based on energy. If neglected, they produce errors in the determination of the elastic moduli. Thus, by comparing Eq. (
85
) with the simplest theory in Eq. (
81
), we see that
C_{1212}
is corrected by the term
\sigma_{*22}
. However, when rotations are absent and Eq. (
87
) is valid,

C_{1212}
is corrected by the term
\frac{1}{4}(\sigma_{*11}+\sigma_{*22})
.

6
RELATIONSHIPS FOR THE INTERMEDIATE CONFIGURATION UNDER HYDROSTATIC PRESSURE

In the treatment that follows, the deformed intermediate configuration under hydrostatic pressure
p
is considered, i.e.,

\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}=-p\boldsymbol{I};\hskip 23.49976pt\sigma_{*ij}=-p\delta_{ij}.

(88)

This substitution can be made for all equations of the previous sections. However, this particular case also allows some qualitatively new results, which are not valid for the general preliminary stress tensor.

For single crystals, the symmetric deformation gradient tensor
\boldsymbol{F}_{*}
that produces the intermediate configuration is described by the experimentally determined function

\boldsymbol{F}_{*}=\boldsymbol{F}_{*}(p),

(89)

which we will call a generalized equation of state.
While in the experiment, depending on the way the crystal is fixed, generally nonsymmetric
\bar{\boldsymbol{F}}_{*}
may be obtained and can be decomposed into the symmetric
\boldsymbol{F}_{*}
and the orthogonal
R
tensors characterizing rigid-body rotation using polar decomposition
\bar{\boldsymbol{F}}_{*}=\mbox{\boldsymbol{$R$}}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}_{*}
; rotation should be excluded because it can be made arbitrary by rotating an observer. Relationship Eq. (
89
), in particular, results in the equation of state

J_{*}=det\boldsymbol{F}_{*}=J_{*}(p).

(90)

For the cubic crystal and isotropic polycrystalline aggregate, Eq. (
90
) is the only scalar equation that follows from Eq. (
89
). For lower lattice symmetries, the number of independent scalar equations in Eq. (
89
) is equal to the number of nonzero components of the transformational deformation gradient that transform the cubic lattice into the lattice of interest during martensitic phase transformation. The transformation matrices for such transformations are collected, e. g., in
[
66
,
67
]
. For example, for tetragonal and hexagonal lattices, there are two independent equations for
F_{*11}=F_{*22}
and
F_{*33}
; for orthorhombic lattices and monoclinic lattices (with the axis of monoclinic symmetry corresponding to
<100>_{cubic}
direction) there are four independent equations (
F_{*13}=F_{*23}=0
only); for
monoclinic lattices (with the axis of monoclinic symmetry corresponding to
<110>_{cubic}
direction) and triclinic lattices, there are all six independent equations.

The elastic energies per unit volume in the reference configuration
\Omega_{0}
and the intermediate configuration
\Omega_{*}
are

\begin{split}\psi_{0}=\psi_{0}(\boldsymbol{E}_{0});\hskip 23.49976pt\psi=\psi_{0}/J_{*}=\psi(0)-p\boldsymbol{I}:\boldsymbol{E}+\tilde{\psi}(\boldsymbol{E}).\end{split}

(91)

Similar to the general initial stress, while
p
has the physical meaning of pressure, in the thermodynamic treatment (e.g., application of thermodynamic laws to derive elasticity rules or temperature evolution equation),
p
should be treated just as a constant in the linear term in Eq. (
91
) assuming the fixed intermediate configuration. The intermediate configuration and
p
can be varied in the final equations. In particular, in the thermodynamic treatment, pressure-dependent elastic moduli should not be differentiated with respect to pressure.

6.1
Stresses and stress rate

The second Piola-Kirchhoff stress in the reference
\boldsymbol{T}_{0}
and intermediate
\boldsymbol{T}
configurations are defined as follows:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{T}_{0}}={J_{0}}\ {\boldsymbol{F}_{0}}^{-1}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot{\boldsymbol{F}_{0}}^{T-1}=\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}};\hskip 23.49976pt\boldsymbol{T}=J\boldsymbol{F}^{-1}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}^{T-1}=\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}}=-p\boldsymbol{I}+\frac{\partial\tilde{\psi}}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}};\hskip 11.74988pt

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{T}_{0}=J_{*}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{-1}_{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{T}\cdot{\boldsymbol{F}}^{T-1}_{*};\hskip 23.49976pt\boldsymbol{T}=J_{*}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{T}_{0}\cdot{\boldsymbol{F}}^{T}_{*},

(92)

where
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
is Cauchy (true) stress. It is clear that for
\boldsymbol{F}=\boldsymbol{I}
one has
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\boldsymbol{T}=-p\boldsymbol{I}
, which justifies presentation Eq. (
91
) for
\psi
.
In the component form

\displaystyle T_{0,ij}={J_{0}}F_{0,ik}^{-1}\sigma_{kl}F_{0,jl}^{-1}=\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial E_{0,ij}};\hskip 23.49976ptT_{ij}={J}F_{ik}^{-1}\sigma_{kl}F_{jl}^{-1}=\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial E_{ij}}=-p\delta_{ij}+\frac{\partial\tilde{\psi}}{\partial E_{ij}};\hskip 11.74988pt

\displaystyle T_{*ij}={J_{*}}F_{*ik}^{-1}T_{kl}F_{*jl}^{-1};\hskip 23.49976ptT_{ij}={J_{*}^{-1}}{F_{*ik}}T_{0,kl}{F_{*jl}}.

(93)

It follows from Eq. (
92
)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}={J_{0}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{F}_{0}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{T}_{0}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{F}_{0}}^{T}={J_{0}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{F}_{0}}\cdot\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{F}_{0}}^{T}\hskip 11.74988pt\rightarrow

\displaystyle@\boldsymbol{F}=\boldsymbol{I}:\hskip 23.49976pt-p\boldsymbol{I}={J_{*}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{*}\cdot\frac{\partial{\psi_{0}}}{\partial{\boldsymbol{E}_{0}}}\mid_{\boldsymbol{E}_{0}=\boldsymbol{E}_{*}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{F}^{T}}_{*}.

(94)

For general anisotropic crystals Eq. (
94
) is the inverse of Eq. (
89
), i.e., the generalized equation of state.
In the component form

\displaystyle\sigma_{ij}=J_{0}^{-1}F_{0,ik}T_{0,kl}F_{0,jl}=J_{0}^{-1}F_{0,ik}\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial E_{0,kl}}F_{0,jl}\rightarrow

\displaystyle@F_{ij}=\delta_{ij}:\hskip 23.49976pt-p\delta_{ij}=J_{*}^{-1}{F}_{*ik}{F}_{*jl}\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial E_{0,kl}}\mid_{E_{0,ab}=E_{*ab}}.

(95)

Also,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}={J}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{F}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{T}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{F}}^{T}={J}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{F}}\cdot\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{F}}^{T}=-p{J}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{F}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{F}}^{T}+{J}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{F}}\cdot\frac{\partial\tilde{\psi}}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{F}}^{T}.

(96)

\displaystyle\sigma_{ij}=J^{-1}F_{ik}T_{kl}F_{jl}=J^{-1}F_{ik}\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial E_{kl}}F_{jl}=-pJ^{-1}F_{ik}F_{jk}+J^{-1}F_{ik}\frac{\partial\tilde{\psi}}{\partial E_{kl}}F_{jl}.

(97)

All equations from Section
2.3
for elastic moduli remain unchanged.
All equations from Section
3
down to Eq. (
36
) remain unchanged because they do not use an intermediate configuration. If we consider in Eqs. (
37
) and (
38
) stress in the current configuration
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=-p\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}
, these equations simplify to

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nabla}}{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{J}=\dot{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}=\bar{\boldsymbol{C}}:\boldsymbol{d}+p(\boldsymbol{I}\boldsymbol{I}:\boldsymbol{d}-2\boldsymbol{d})=(\bar{\boldsymbol{C}}+p(\boldsymbol{I}\boldsymbol{I}-2\boldsymbol{I}^{4}_{s}):\boldsymbol{d}=\boldsymbol{B}:\boldsymbol{d};
