\displaystyle\dot{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}=\boldsymbol{B}:\boldsymbol{d};\hskip 23.49976pt\boldsymbol{B}:=\bar{\boldsymbol{C}}+p(\boldsymbol{I}\boldsymbol{I}-2\boldsymbol{I}^{4}_{s});

(98)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nabla}}{{\sigma}}_{ij}=\dot{\sigma}_{ij}=\bar{C}_{ijkl}d_{lk}+p(\delta_{ij}\delta_{mn}d_{nm}-2d_{ij})=(\bar{C}_{ijkl}d_{lk}+p(\delta_{ij}\delta_{kl}-\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}-\delta_{il}\delta_{jk})d_{kl};

\displaystyle\dot{\sigma}_{ij}=B_{ijkl}d_{kl};\hskip 23.49976ptB_{ijkl}:=\bar{C}_{ijkl}d_{lk}+p(\delta_{ij}\delta_{kl}-\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}-\delta_{il}\delta_{jk}).

(99)

We took into account that
-(\boldsymbol{w}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}^{T})=p(\boldsymbol{w}+\boldsymbol{w}^{T})=0
, i.e., the spin tensor does not contribute to the stress rate for initially hydrostatically loaded crystal. Based on the definition, we see that in contrast to the general stress state at time
t
, for the initial hydrostatic loading tensor
B_{ijkl}=B_{klij}
. Thus, for this case, the tensor
\boldsymbol{B}
has full Voigt symmetry. Also, hydrostatic pressure does not change the symmetry of the stress-free crystal lattice, which significantly simplifies analysis.
Eq. (
98
)
can be integrated for small time increment:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(t+\Delta t)=-p(t)\boldsymbol{I}+\boldsymbol{B}:\dot{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}\Delta t;\hskip 11.74988pt{\sigma}_{ij}(t+\Delta t)=-p(t)\delta_{ij}+B_{ijkl}\dot{\epsilon}_{lk}\Delta t.

(100)

The application of this equation is limited to the case when, for the next time step, stress at the previous time step is hydrostatic. This is the case if the entire loading is hydrostatic, which is of limited interest.
However, based on
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(t+\Delta t)
, one can update pressure
p(t+\Delta t)
and using Eq. (
89
), update
\boldsymbol{F}_{*}(t+\Delta t)
and the intermediate configuration. This configuration under updated pressure can be used as
a new intermediate configuration
\Omega_{*}
for the next time step and strain increment
\dot{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}\Delta t
should be calculated with respect to this configuration.

If an intermediate configuration
\Omega_{*}
is updated, then strain
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
is evaluated with respect to updated configuration
\Omega_{*}
without remembering previous values of
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
, Eq. (
100
)
can be presented in terms of
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}=\Delta\boldsymbol{\epsilon}=\dot{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}\Delta t

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}=-p(\boldsymbol{F}_{*})\boldsymbol{I}+\boldsymbol{B}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon};\hskip 23.49976pt\boldsymbol{\epsilon}=\boldsymbol{F}_{*}^{-1T}(p){\cdot}(\boldsymbol{E}_{0}-\boldsymbol{E}_{*}(p)){\cdot}\boldsymbol{F}_{*}^{-1}(p);

(101)

\displaystyle{\sigma}_{ij}=-p(\boldsymbol{F}_{*mn})\delta_{ij}+B_{ijkl}\epsilon_{lk};\hskip 23.49976pt\epsilon_{lk}=F_{*ik}^{-1}(p)(E_{0,ij}-E_{*ij}(p))F_{*jl}^{-1}(p),

(102)

where Eqs. (
4
) and (
5
) were utilized. Both equations are nonlinearly connected, since
p
depends on
\boldsymbol{F}_{*}
in the first equation and
\boldsymbol{F}_{*}
depends on
p
in the second equation, and should be solved iteratively, if
\boldsymbol{E}_{0}
is prescribed.
Of course, one can use Eq. (
45
) with general
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
dependent expression for the tensor
\boldsymbol{B}
in Eq. (
40
). However, the advantage of utilizing Eq. (
100
)
is that
the tensor
\boldsymbol{B}
has full Voigt symmetry and the symmetry of the stress-free crystal lattice, which significantly simplifies analysis.

6.2
Consistency conditions utilizing single crystal data

Here, we will find which constraints on the elastic compliances and moduli are imposed from the experimental generalized equation of state (
89
) under hydrostatic loading, which is usually obtained using an x-ray diffraction measurement of the change of lattice parameters under hydrostatic pressure; we will call them the consistency conditions.
We will consider two cases: (a) When the generalized equation of state is obtained from the experiment on a single crystal and (b)
when it is extracted from the polycrystalline sample.

One of the universal consistency conditions for any crystal symmetry can be immediately found from the equation of state (
90
) when the intermediate configuration coincides with the current one: it defines the bulk modulus
K
and compliance
k
, which are connected to elastic moduli and compliances by Eq. (
57
) and Eq. (
63
), respectively:

\displaystyle{K}_{V}=-J_{*}\frac{\partial p}{\partial J_{*}}|_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{dev}}=-\frac{\partial p}{\partial\ln J_{*}}|_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{dev}}=1/9\,\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{B}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}=1/9\,B_{iijj};

(103)

\displaystyle k_{R}=-\frac{\partial\ln J_{*}}{\partial p}|_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{dev}}=\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{\lambda}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}=\lambda_{iijj}.

(104)

Eq. (
103
) or (
104
) is routinely used to check correctness of the determined elastic moduli or helps to find
n
elastic moduli from
n-1
experiments or atomistic simulations that do not involve
K
or
k
.
Note that Eqs. (
103
) and (
104
) are not equivalent.

Below we will derive all consistency conditions related to the generalized equation of state (
89
) under hydrostatic loading.
When
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
represents hydrostatic state of stress, Eq. (
98
) reduces to the following equation:

\displaystyle-\dot{p}\boldsymbol{I}=\boldsymbol{B}(p):\boldsymbol{d}.

(105)

6.2.1
Consistency conditions for elastic compliances

Inverting Eq. (
105
) by double contraction with elastic compliance
\boldsymbol{\lambda}(p)
(
\boldsymbol{\lambda}(p):\boldsymbol{B}(p)=\boldsymbol{I}^{4}_{s}
) gives:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{d}=-\boldsymbol{\lambda}(p):\boldsymbol{I}\dot{p}.

(106)

From the other side, it follows from Eq. (
89
)

\boldsymbol{d}_{*}:=(\dot{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$F$}}_{*}}{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$\cdot$}}}{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$F$}}_{*}}^{-1})_{s}=\left(\frac{d\boldsymbol{F}_{*}(p)}{dp}{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$\cdot$}}}{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$F$}}}_{*}^{-1}\right)_{s}\dot{p}.

(107)

Since under hydrostatic loading
\boldsymbol{d}=\boldsymbol{d}_{*}
, by comparing Eqs. (
106
) and Eq. (
107
) , we obtain

\boldsymbol{\lambda}(p):\boldsymbol{I}=-\left(\frac{d\boldsymbol{F}_{*}(p)}{dp}{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$\cdot$}}}{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$F$}}}_{*}^{-1}\right)_{s}\hskip 11.74988pt\rightarrow\hskip 11.74988pt\lambda_{ijkk}=-\left(\frac{d{F_{*}}_{im}(p)}{dp}{F_{*}}_{mj}^{-1}\right)_{s}.

(108)

Eq. (
108
), which we call a consistency condition for elastic compliances, imposes linear constraints on the elastic compliances coming from the experimental generalized equation of state (
89
) under hydrostatic loading. The number of constraints is equal to the number
of independent strain components that appear under hydrostatic pressure for a given symmetry of a lattice. It equals to the number of independent lattice parameters or components of the transformation strain tensor for phase transformation from the cubic to the given lattice.
Thus, it is two for hexagonal, trigonal, rhombohedral, and tetragonal lattices, three for orthorhombic, four for monoclinic, and six for triclinic.

In particular, the trace of Eq. (
108
) in combination with the definition (
63
) of the bulk compliance
k_{R}
leads to

\displaystyle k_{R}=\lambda_{iijj}=\boldsymbol{I}:\boldsymbol{\lambda}(p):\boldsymbol{I}=-\frac{d\boldsymbol{F}_{*}(p)}{dp}:{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$F$}}}_{*}^{-1}=-\frac{d{F_{*}}_{im}(p)}{dp}{F_{*}}_{mi}^{-1}.

(109)

Since
dJ_{*}/J_{*}=d\ln J_{*}=d\boldsymbol{F}_{*}:{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$F$}}}_{*}^{-1}=d{F_{*}}_{im}{F_{*}}_{mi}^{-1}

[
63
,
52
]
, Eq. (
109
) is equivalent to Eq. (
104
).
For example, if
\boldsymbol{F}_{*}
is a diagonal tensor (for orthorhombic lattices), then Eq. (
108
) takes the following form

\displaystyle\lambda_{11kk}=-\frac{1}{{F_{*}}_{11}}\frac{d{F_{*}}_{11}(p)}{dp}=-\frac{d\ln\left[{F_{*}}_{11}(p)\right]}{dp};\hskip 11.74988pt\lambda_{22kk}=-\frac{1}{{F_{*}}_{22}}\frac{d{F_{*}}_{22}(p)}{dp}=-\frac{d\ln\left[{F_{*}}_{22}(p)\right]}{dp};

\displaystyle\lambda_{33kk}=-\frac{1}{{F_{*}}_{33}}\frac{d{F_{*}}_{33}(p)}{dp}=-\frac{d\ln\left[{F_{*}}_{33}(p)\right]}{dp}.

(110)

Recall, that
\lambda_{11kk}
,
\lambda_{22kk}
, and
\lambda_{33kk}
are linear compliances along the orthorhombic axes
[
68
]
.
For hexagonal and tetragonal systems
{F_{*}}_{11}={F_{*}}_{22}
and
\lambda_{11kk}=\lambda_{22kk}
, and nontrivial Eqs. (
110
) in more explicit form are

\lambda_{1111}+\lambda_{1122}+\lambda_{1133}=-\frac{d\ln\left[{F_{*}}_{11}(p)\right]}{dp};\hskip 11.74988pt\lambda_{3311}+\lambda_{3322}+\lambda_{3333}=-\frac{d\ln\left[{F_{*}}_{33}(p)\right]}{dp}.

(111)

With Voigt designation, Eq. (
111
) looks like

\lambda_{11}+\lambda_{12}+\lambda_{13}=-\frac{d\ln\left[{F_{*}}_{1}(p)\right]}{dp};\hskip 11.74988pt2\lambda_{13}+\lambda_{33}=-\frac{d\ln\left[{F_{*}}_{3}(p)\right]}{dp},

(112)

where symmetry
\lambda_{32}=\lambda_{23}
and
\lambda_{23}=\lambda_{13}
is taken into account.

6.2.2
Consistency condition for elastic moduli

Let substitute in Eq. (
105
)
deformation rate
\boldsymbol{d}_{*}
for hydrostatic loading from Eq. (
107
):

\displaystyle-\dot{p}\boldsymbol{I}=\boldsymbol{B}(p):\left(\frac{d\boldsymbol{F}_{*}(p)}{dp}{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$\cdot$}}}{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$F$}}}_{*}^{-1}\right)_{s}\dot{p}\hskip 11.74988pt\rightarrow\hskip 11.74988pt\boldsymbol{B}(p):\left(\frac{d\boldsymbol{F}_{*}(p)}{dp}{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$\cdot$}}}{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$F$}}}_{*}^{-1}\right)_{s}=-\boldsymbol{I}\hskip 11.74988pt\rightarrow\hskip 11.74988pt

\displaystyle B_{ijkl}(p)\left(\frac{d{F_{*}}_{lm}(p)}{dp}{F_{*}}^{-1}_{mk}\right)_{s}=-\delta_{ij},

(113)

where symmetrization can be omitted due to symmetry of
B_{ijkl}
in
k
and
l
.
Eq. (
113
) represents the desired linear constraints on elastic moduli.

As an example, for orthorhombic lattices Eq. (
113
) simplifies to

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{B}(p):\frac{d\ln\left[\boldsymbol{F}_{*}(p)\right]}{dp}=-\boldsymbol{I};\hskip 23.49976ptB_{ijkk}(p)\frac{d\ln\left[{F_{*}}_{kk}(p)\right]}{dp}=-\delta_{ij}.

(114)

For hexagonal and tetragonal crystals Eq. (
114
) reduces to

\displaystyle[B_{11}(p)+B_{12}(p))]\frac{d\ln\left[{F_{*}}_{11}(p)\right]}{dp}+B_{13}(p)\frac{d\ln\left[{F_{*}}_{33}(p)\right]}{dp}=-1;

\displaystyle 2B_{13}(p)\frac{d\ln\left[{F_{*}}_{11}(p)\right]}{dp}+B_{33}(p)\frac{d\ln\left[{F_{*}}_{33}(p)\right]}{dp}=-1.

(115)

The difference between consistency conditions for compliance and moduli is that the former constrains the sum of some compliances, but the latter
constrains the weighted sum of elastic moduli.

Let us express for comparison the constraints on compliances Eq. (
112
) for hexagonal and tetragonal lattices in terms of elastic moduli.
We recollect relationships between
\lambda_{ij}
and
B_{ij}
for hexagonal and tetragonal (classes
4mm
,
\bar{4}2m
,
42m
, and
4/mmm
) systems
[
68
]
:

\displaystyle\lambda_{11}+\lambda_{12}=B_{33}/B;\hskip 11.74988pt\lambda_{13}=-B_{13}/B;\hskip 11.74988pt\lambda_{33}=(B_{11}+B_{12})/B;\hskip 11.74988pt

\displaystyle B=B_{33}(B_{11}+B_{12})-2B_{13}^{2}.

(116)

Substituting them in Eq. (
112
), we obtain consistency conditions for compliances expressed in terms of elastic moduli:

\frac{B_{33}-B_{13}}{B}=-\frac{d\ln\left[{F_{*}}_{1}(p)\right]}{dp};\hskip 11.74988pt\frac{B_{11}+B_{12}-2B_{13}}{B}=-\frac{d\ln\left[{F_{*}}_{3}(p)\right]}{dp}.

(117)

They are equivalent to Eq. (
115
) and can be obtained from Eq. (
115
) by solving them for

\frac{d\ln\left[{F_{*}}_{1}(p)\right]}{dp}
and
\frac{d\ln\left[{F_{*}}_{3}(p)\right]}{dp}
.
Due to non-linearity of these constraints in terms of
B_{ij}
, their application is less convenient than in Eq. (
115
) or Eq. (
112
) for
\lambda_{ij}
.

Equivalence of the consistency conditions
for elastic moduli and compliances can be proven in the general case. Indeed, producing double contraction of both sides of Eq. (
108
) with
\boldsymbol{B}
we obtain

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{B}:\boldsymbol{\lambda}(p):\boldsymbol{I}=\boldsymbol{I}^{4}_{s}:\boldsymbol{I}=\boldsymbol{I}=-\boldsymbol{B}:\left(\frac{d\boldsymbol{F}_{*}(p)}{dp}{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$\cdot$}}}{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$F$}}}_{*}^{-1}\right)_{s};

\displaystyle B_{ijab}\lambda_{abkk}=I^{4}_{s,ijkk}=\delta_{ij}=-B_{ijab}\left(\frac{d{F_{*}}_{am}(p)}{dp}{F_{*}}_{mb}^{-1}\right)_{s},

(118)

which coincides with Eq. (
113
). We can also present Eq. (
108
) in terms of elastic moduli

\boldsymbol{B}^{-1}(p):\boldsymbol{I}=-\left(\frac{d\boldsymbol{F}_{*}(p)}{dp}{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$\cdot$}}}{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$F$}}}_{*}^{-1}\right)_{s};\hskip 23.49976ptB^{-1}_{ijkk}=-\left(\frac{d{F_{*}}_{im}(p)}{dp}{F_{*}}_{mj}^{-1}\right)_{s}

(119)

and Eq. (
113
) in terms of elastic compliances

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{-1}(p):\left(\frac{d\boldsymbol{F}_{*}(p)}{dp}{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$\cdot$}}}{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$F$}}}_{*}^{-1}\right)_{s}=-\boldsymbol{I};\hskip 23.49976pt\lambda^{-1}_{ijkl}(p)\left(\frac{d{F_{*}}_{lm}(p)}{dp}{F_{*}}^{-1}_{mk}\right)_{s}=-\delta_{ij}.

(120)

Due to inversion, these are nonlinear constraints on the elastic moduli and compliances, which are less convenient than the original linear constraints (
108
) and (
113
).

6.2.3
Constraint involving the bulk modulus

As a particular case of Eq. (
113
), let us try to derive the constraint related to the definition of the bulk modulus
K_{V}
the way we did for bulk compliance, i.e. by finding trace of the consistency condition. First, with the help of the definition of the deformation rate (
30
), when the current configuration coincides with the intermediate configuration,

\boldsymbol{d}_{*}=\left(\dot{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$F$}}}_{*}\cdot\mbox{\boldsymbol{$F$}}^{-1}_{*}\right)_{s}
and its decomposition (
54
)
into spherical and deviatoric parts
\boldsymbol{d}_{*}=\frac{1}{3}\dot{\varepsilon}_{0*}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}+\boldsymbol{d}_{dev*}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left(\frac{d\boldsymbol{F}_{*}(p)}{dp}{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$\cdot$}}}{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$F$}}}_{*}^{-1}\right)_{s}=\boldsymbol{d}_{*}\frac{dt}{dp}=(\frac{1}{3}\dot{\varepsilon}_{0*}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}+\boldsymbol{d}_{dev*})\frac{dt}{dp};

\displaystyle\left(\frac{d{F_{*}}_{im}(p)}{dp}{F_{*}}^{-1}_{mj}\right)_{s}=d_{*ij}\frac{dt}{dp}=(\frac{1}{3}\dot{\varepsilon}_{0*}\delta_{ij}+d_{dev*,ij})\frac{dt}{dp}.

(121)

Then finding the trace of Eq. (
113
), we derive

\displaystyle-3=\boldsymbol{I}:\boldsymbol{B}(p):\left(\frac{1}{3}\dot{\varepsilon}_{0*}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}+\boldsymbol{d}_{dev*}\right)\frac{dt}{dp}=\left(3K_{V}\dot{\varepsilon}_{0*}+\boldsymbol{I}:\boldsymbol{B}(p):\boldsymbol{d}_{dev*}\right)\frac{dt}{dp}=

\displaystyle 3K_{V}\frac{d{\varepsilon}_{0*}}{dp}|_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{dev}}+\boldsymbol{I}:\boldsymbol{B}(p):\left(\frac{d\boldsymbol{F}_{*}(p)}{dp}{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$\cdot$}}}{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$F$}}}_{*}^{-1}\right)_{dev};

\displaystyle-3=B_{iikl}(p)\left(\frac{1}{3}\dot{\varepsilon}_{*0}\delta_{kl}+d_{dev*,kl}\right)\frac{dt}{dp}=\left(3K_{V}\dot{\varepsilon}_{0*}+B_{iikl}(p)d_{dev*,kl}\right)\frac{dt}{dp}=

\displaystyle 3K_{V}\frac{d{\varepsilon}_{0*}}{dp}|_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{dev}}+B_{iikl}(p)\left(\frac{d{F_{*}}_{km}(p)}{dp}{F_{*}}^{-1}_{ml}\right)_{dev}.

(122)

We used definition Eq. (
57
) of the bulk modulus
K_{V}=1/9\,\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{B}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}=1/9\,B_{iijj}
and also that pressure derivatives are evaluated at fixed
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{dev}
and
\boldsymbol{\omega}
, like in the initial Eq. (
113
) .
With
\varepsilon_{0*}:={\ln J_{*}}=det{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$F$}}}_{*}
from Eq. (
52
) for the current configuration coinciding with the intermediate configuration, Eq. (
122
) can be presented in the form

\displaystyle K_{V}\frac{d\ln J_{*}}{dp}|_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{dev}}+\frac{1}{3}\boldsymbol{I}:\boldsymbol{B}(p):\left(\frac{d\boldsymbol{F}_{*}(p)}{dp}{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$\cdot$}}}{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$F$}}}_{*}^{-1}\right)_{dev}=-1;

\displaystyle K_{V}\frac{d\ln J_{*}}{dp}|_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{dev}}+\frac{1}{3}B_{iikl}(p)\left(\frac{d{F_{*}}_{km}(p)}{dp}{F_{*}}^{-1}_{ml}\right)_{dev}=-1.

(123)

Eq. (
123
) is clearly not equivalent to the constraint (
103
) on the bulk modulus
K_{V}
, because it explicitly depends on the deviatoric part

\left(\frac{d\boldsymbol{F}_{*}(p)}{dp}{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$\cdot$}}}{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$F$}}}_{*}^{-1}\right)_{dev}
.
However, this is noncontradictory because careful utilization of the definitions leads to

\displaystyle K_{V}\frac{d\ln J_{*}}{dp}|_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{dev}}=-\frac{dp}{d\ln J_{*}}|_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{dev}}\frac{d\ln J_{*}}{dp}|_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{dev}}\neq-1,

(124)

because derivatives are evaluated for different and nonequivalent constraints. Thus, Eq. (
123
) represents noncontradictory
constraint on
\boldsymbol{I}:\boldsymbol{B}(p):\left(\frac{d\boldsymbol{F}_{*}(p)}{dp}{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$\cdot$}}}{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$F$}}}_{*}^{-1}\right)_{dev}
, which is part of the general constraints (
113
).

If we would neglect this difference and insert
K_{V}\frac{d\ln J_{*}}{dp}=-\frac{dp}{d\ln J_{*}}\frac{d\ln J_{*}}{dp}=-1
in Eq. (
123
) , we would obtain

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{I}:\boldsymbol{B}(p):\left(\frac{d\boldsymbol{F}_{*}(p)}{dp}{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$\cdot$}}}{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$F$}}}_{*}^{-1}\right)_{dev}=B_{iikl}(p)\left(\frac{d{F_{*}}_{km}(p)}{dp}{F_{*}}^{-1}_{ml}\right)_{dev}=0\rightarrow{\rm nonsense},

(125)

because it does not follow from the general constraints (
113
). This became more evident, e.g., for hexagonal and tetragonal
crystals, for which
(\ln F_{*11})_{dev}=(\ln F_{*22})_{dev}=-\frac{1}{2}(\ln F_{*33})_{dev}
, and (
125
) simplifies to

\displaystyle B_{iikl}(p)\left(\frac{d{F_{*}}_{km}(p)}{dp}{F_{*}}^{-1}_{ml}\right)_{dev}=B_{iikk}(p)\left(\frac{d\ln[{F_{*}}_{kk}(p)]}{dp}\right)_{dev}=

\displaystyle(B_{ii11}(p)+B_{ii22}(p)-2B_{ii33}(p))\left(\frac{d\ln[{F_{*}}_{11}(p)]}{dp}\right)_{dev}=

\displaystyle 2(B_{11}(p)+B_{12}(p)-B_{13}(p)-B_{33}(p))\left(\frac{d\ln[{F_{*}}_{11}(p)]}{dp}\right)_{dev}=0\rightarrow

\displaystyle B_{11}(p)+B_{12}(p)-B_{13}(p)-B_{33}(p)=0\rightarrow{\rm nonsense},

(126)

because there is no relationship between these 4 elastic moduli for tetragonal and hexagonal lattices.
Thus, constraint (
103
) on the elastic moduli expressed in terms of bulk modulus
K_{V}=1/9\,\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{B}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}=1/9\,B_{iijj}
cannot be directly derived from the trace (
56
) of the general constraint(
55
). This is in contrast to the constraint (
104
) on elastic compliances expressed in terms of bulk compliance
k_{R}=\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{\lambda}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}=\lambda_{iijj}
, because it is strictly derived in Eq. (
109
) from the trace of the general consistency condition for elastic compliances Eq. (
108
).

The difference between these results for bulk modulus and compressibility are in general equations (
56
) and (
62
) and the fact that consistency conditions are based on the relationship between hydrostatic pressure
p
and some deformation gradient
\boldsymbol{F}_{*}
, which includes anisotropic strain.
For hydrostatic loading Eq. (
62
) gives

\displaystyle\dot{\varepsilon}_{0}=-\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{\lambda}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}\dot{p}=k_{R}\dot{p}\rightarrow k_{R}=-\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{\lambda}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}.

(127)

However, for hydrostatic loading we cannot set in Eq. (
56
)

\dot{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{dev}=0
and receive
K_{V}={1}/{9}\,\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{B}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}
; instead, we obtain the more general Eq. (
123
).
Expression
K_{V}={1}/{9}\,\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{B}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}
is derived independently in Eq. (
103
) under nonhydrostatic stresses but fixed
{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{dev}
.

Thus, either of constraints on bulk moduli,
K_{V}={1}/{9}\,\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{B}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}
or
K_{R}=\frac{1}{k_{R}}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{iijj}}
can be applied as one of the equations for finding all elastic moduli, provided that
K_{V}
and
K_{R}
are correctly determined from the experiments or atomic simulations. Modulus
K_{V}
is easier to calculate by prescribing isotropic expansion to lattice, calculating energy, the stress tensor and pressure and using
{K}_{V}=-V\frac{\partial p}{\partial V}|_{{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{dev}}=V^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}{\psi_{c}}}{\partial V^{2}}|_{\boldsymbol{F}=J^{1/3}\boldsymbol{I}}=-\frac{\partial p}{\partial{\varepsilon}_{0}}|_{{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{dev}}
. Calculation of
K_{R}
should be produced under hydrostatic loading, i.e., lattice parameters under fixed volume should be varied to relax all deviatoric stresses, which requires much more calculations. Then
{K}_{R}=-V\frac{\partial p}{\partial V}|_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{dev}}=V^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}{\psi_{c}}}{\partial V^{2}}|_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{dev}=0}=-\frac{\partial p}{\partial{\varepsilon}_{0}}|_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{dev}}
. Also,
K_{V}
imposes linear constraint on
\boldsymbol{B}
components, but
K_{R}
represents a nonlinear constraint. Both these results demonstrate some advantage of using
K_{V}
-related constraint in comparison with
K_{V}
-related constraint. However, relaxation of deviatoric stresses for each pressure should be performed in any case to find all other elastic moduli under pressure.

6.3
Consistency conditions utilizing data from polycrystalline sample

Utilizing function
\boldsymbol{F}_{*}(p)
. While for single crystal the loading is clear, i.e., pressure is applied, and deformation is measured, for polycrystalline aggregates, due to different orientation and interaction of crystals, there is a distribution of stress and strains.
For applied pressure,
p
, in gas or liquid, each crystal of a polycrystalline aggregate possesses its own stress tensor
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
, which causes own
\boldsymbol{F}_{*}
in the crystallographic axes of each crystal.
X-ray diffraction measures averaged over the aggregate change in lattice parameters.
That is, in a thought experiment, all crystals are rotated to the same orientation of their cells, like for a single crystal, then each component of
\boldsymbol{F}_{*}
is averaged over the aggregate and is related to the averaged pressure in a polycrystal, which is equal to pressure
p
in a liquid. Then dependence
\boldsymbol{F}_{*}(p)
in terms of averaged
\boldsymbol{F}_{*}
and
p
can be used in equations of Section

6.2

the same way as for a single crystal.

This approach is simple, straightforward, and differs from the case when one uses some
effective properties of the polycrystalline aggregate instead of
\boldsymbol{F}_{*}(p)
, e.g., effective bulk and shear moduli for isotropic polycrystal in terms of elastic moduli of a single crystal.

Utilizing effective elastic properties.

There are numerous methods of determining the effective elastic properties of heterogeneous materials.
The simplest way to determine the effective elastic properties is to assume that all crystals have the same strain or distortion (Voigt approximation
[
69
,
70
,
65
]
), for which averaging is performed for elastic moduli
\boldsymbol{B}_{V}
and the same stress (Reuss approximation
[
64
,
70
,
65
]
), for which averaging is performed for
elastic compliances
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{R}
. For isotropic polycrystals, the effective bulk modulus
K_{p}
and shear modulus
\mu_{p}
are bounded by

\displaystyle K_{R}=1/k_{R}\leq K_{p}\leq K_{V};\hskip 23.49976pt\mu_{R}=1/s_{R}\leq\mu_{p}\leq\mu_{V},

(128)

where
s
is shear compliance. According to
[
70
]
, a good approximation is

\displaystyle K_{p}=K_{H}=\frac{1}{2}(K_{V}+K_{R});\hskip 23.49976pt\mu_{p}=\mu_{H}=\frac{1}{2}(\mu_{V}+\mu_{R}).

(129)

It is clear that these equations are valid for any pressure.
For cubic crystals only
K_{H}=K_{V}=K_{R}
. For effective anisotropic (textured) polycrystalline aggregate, similar lower Reuss and upper Voigt bounds on elastic moduli can be formulated in an energetic sense,
and one of the possible Hill-type averaging is

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{B}_{p}(p)=\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{B}_{V}(p)+\boldsymbol{B}_{R}(p));\hskip 23.49976pt\boldsymbol{B}_{R}(p)=\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{-1}_{R}(p),

(130)

see e.g.,
[
71
]
.
For triclinic crystals

\displaystyle{K}_{V}=1/9\,B_{iijj}=1/9\,(B_{11}+B_{22}+B_{33}+2(B_{12}+B_{23}+B_{13}));

\displaystyle k_{R}=\lambda_{iijj}=\lambda_{11}+\lambda_{22}+\lambda_{33}+2(\lambda_{12}+\lambda_{23}+\lambda_{13}).

\displaystyle\mu_{V}=1/15\,(B_{11}+B_{22}+B_{33}-(B_{12}+B_{23}+B_{13})+3(B_{44}+B_{55}+B_{66}));

\displaystyle 15/\mu_{R}=4(\lambda_{11}+\lambda_{22}+\lambda_{33})-4(\lambda_{12}+\lambda_{23}+\lambda_{13})+3(\lambda_{44}+\lambda_{55}+\lambda_{66}).

(131)

While for single crystal we do not produce any averaging, Eqs. (
103
) and (
104
) for single crystals coincide with Eq. (
131
) for
K_{V}
and
k_{R}
for polycrystalline aggregate.
This is because it is easy to show that
K_{V}
and
k_{R}
in Eqs. (
103
) and (
104
) for arbitrary crystals are independent of crystal orientation.
That is why we added subscripts
V
and
R
for single crystals to underline that
K_{V}
is based on the Voigt approximation, and
k_{R}
is based on the Reuss approximation.

For higher-symmetry crystals, Eqs. (
131
) trivially simplifies.
For example, for tetragonal and hexagonal single crystals, we obtain from Eq. (
131
) for bulk modulus and bulk compliance

\displaystyle{K}_{V}=1/9\,(2B_{11}+2B_{12}+4B_{13}+B_{33});

\displaystyle k_{R}=2\lambda_{11}+2\lambda_{12}+4\lambda_{13}+\lambda_{33}=\frac{B_{11}+B_{12}-4B_{13}+2B_{33}}{B_{33}(B_{11}+B_{12})-2B_{13}^{2}}\rightarrow

\displaystyle K_{R}=\frac{1}{k_{R}}=\frac{B_{33}(B_{11}+B_{12})-2B_{13}^{2}}{B_{11}+B_{12}-4B_{13}+2B_{33}},

(132)

where Eq. (
116
) was used.
Using expressions for
K_{V}
,
K_{R}
,
\mu_{V}
, and
\mu_{R}
, one can find expressions for
K_{H}
and
\mu_{H}
, and then by equaling it to experimental value
K_{p}
, obtain the desired two constraint equations on elastic moduli of single crystals.
Reuss, Voigt, and Hill averages for bulk or/and shear moduli were arbitrarily and routinely used in different papers as one or two of the equations for determination of all elastic moduli, and of course, results depend on which approximation for
K_{p}
and
\mu_{p}
is chosen.
Thus, in contrast to a more detailed and precise approach based on the experimental function
\boldsymbol{F}_{*}(p)
, approaches utilizing effective elastic properties of polycrystalline sample for finding elastic moduli of single crystals always include an error related to the chosen theory for effective elastic moduli. Thus, the approach based on
\boldsymbol{F}_{*}(p)
is much more preferred. Then Eq. (
131
) can be used for comparison of theoretically predicted elastic moduli of polycrystal at different pressures with experiment.

On the other hand, Eq. (
131
) can be used to find pressure-dependent elastic moduli for polycrystalline aggregates.
They also can be used to calibrate some nonlinear isotropic potentials, like Murnaghan potential (
[
72
,
63
]
),
which is broadly used for simulation of deformation processes under high pressure (e.g.,
[
4
,
5
]
).
Murnaghan potential possesses 5 elastic constants, four of which, bulk and shear moduli and their pressure derivatives, can be found
from elastic moduli of single crystals and their pressure derivative.

6.4
Expressions for small distortions in the intermediate configuration

6.4.1
Expression for the elastic energy

Limiting ourselves to quadratic terms in
\boldsymbol{E}
in
\psi
, we obtain for the energy per unit volume in the intermediate configuration
\Omega_{*}
from Eq. (
68
):

\psi=\psi_{0}/J_{*}=\psi(0)-p\boldsymbol{I}:\boldsymbol{E}+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{E}:\boldsymbol{C}:\boldsymbol{E}.

(133)

Utilizing

\boldsymbol{F}=\boldsymbol{I}+\boldsymbol{\beta}=\boldsymbol{I}+\boldsymbol{\epsilon}+\boldsymbol{\omega};\hskip 23.49976pt\boldsymbol{\epsilon}:=(\boldsymbol{\beta})_{s};\hskip 11.74988pt\boldsymbol{\omega}:=(\boldsymbol{\beta})_{a}

(134)

and Eq. (
67
),

\boldsymbol{E}=\boldsymbol{\epsilon}+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{T}\cdot\boldsymbol{\beta}=\boldsymbol{\epsilon}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{\epsilon}+2(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{\omega})_{s}+\boldsymbol{\omega}^{T}\cdot\boldsymbol{\omega}\right),

(135)

we evaluate

\boldsymbol{I}:\boldsymbol{E}=\boldsymbol{\epsilon}:\boldsymbol{I}+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{T}:\boldsymbol{\beta}=\epsilon_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon}+\boldsymbol{\omega}^{T}:\boldsymbol{\omega}\right);\hskip 11.74988pt\epsilon_{0}:=\boldsymbol{\epsilon}:\boldsymbol{I}.

(136)

For small strains and rotations with respect to the intermediate configuration
\Omega_{*}
,
substituting Eq. (
135
) and (
136
) in the expression for the elastic energy
in Eq. (
133
), we obtain in quadratic in distortions approximation

\displaystyle\psi-\psi(0)=-p[\boldsymbol{\epsilon}:\boldsymbol{I}+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{T}:\boldsymbol{\beta}]+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{T}:\boldsymbol{C}:\boldsymbol{\beta}^{T}=-p\boldsymbol{\epsilon}:\boldsymbol{I}+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{T}:\tilde{\boldsymbol{C}}:\boldsymbol{\beta}^{T}=

\displaystyle-p\epsilon_{kk}+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{C}_{ijkl}\beta_{ij}\beta_{kl};

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{C}}:=\boldsymbol{C}-p\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}^{4}_{t};\hskip 23.49976pt\tilde{C}_{ijkl}:=C_{ijkl}-p\delta_{jl}\delta_{ik}.

(137)

Note that if higher order terms in
\boldsymbol{E}
will be utilized in Eq. (
133
), pressure
p
will not contribute to the higher order terms in
\boldsymbol{\beta}
, because
\boldsymbol{E}
does not contain higher than the second-order terms in
\boldsymbol{\beta}
.

After decomposition of
\boldsymbol{\beta}
into
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
and
\boldsymbol{\omega}
in Eq. (
137
), we obtain more detailed expression

\displaystyle\psi-\psi(0)=-p[\boldsymbol{\epsilon}:\boldsymbol{I}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon}+\boldsymbol{\omega}^{T}:\boldsymbol{\omega}\right)]+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}:\boldsymbol{C}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=-p\left(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}:\boldsymbol{I}+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\omega}^{T}:\boldsymbol{\omega}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}:(\boldsymbol{C}-p\boldsymbol{I}^{4}_{s}):\boldsymbol{\epsilon},

(138)

\displaystyle\psi-\psi(0)=-p[\epsilon_{kk}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\epsilon_{ij}\epsilon_{ij}+\omega_{ij}\omega_{ij}\right)]+\frac{1}{2}{C}_{ijkl}\epsilon_{ij}\epsilon_{kl}

\displaystyle=-p\left(\epsilon_{kk}+\frac{1}{2}\omega_{ij}\omega_{ij}\right)+\frac{1}{2}[{C}_{ijkl}-\frac{1}{2}p\left(\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}+\delta_{il}\delta_{jk}\right)]\epsilon_{ij}\epsilon_{kl}.

(139)

A delicate moment in consistent keeping in Eq. (
138
) all quadratic in distortions terms is that in linear in
\boldsymbol{E}
term we keep all quadratic terms but in quadratic in
\boldsymbol{E}
term we retain linear term
\boldsymbol{E}=\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
only. This modifies elastic moduli
\boldsymbol{C}
by
-p\boldsymbol{I}^{4}_{s}
and also shows that the elastic energy depends on the small rotations
\boldsymbol{\omega}
. Small rotations may be related to
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
for specific loadings (e.g., for simple shear), thus also changing elastic moduli.
Also, distortion
\boldsymbol{\beta}
or strain
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
may include higher order components, (e.g.,
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}=\frac{1}{2}\gamma(\boldsymbol{m}\boldsymbol{n}+\boldsymbol{n}\boldsymbol{m}+\frac{\gamma}{1-\gamma^{2}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$k$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$k$}})
(which, as we will discuss later, represents at
\boldsymbol{\omega}=\mbox{\boldsymbol{$0$}}
a isochoric rotation-free shear). Here,
k
is the unit vector orthogonal to
\boldsymbol{n}
and
\boldsymbol{m}
. Then the quadratic part of
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}:\boldsymbol{I}
,
namely,
\frac{\gamma^{2}}{1-\gamma^{2}}\simeq\gamma^{2}
will effectively contribute to elastic moduli. We will discuss this later in more detail. The same is generally true for the general stress tensor
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}
in the intermediate configuration.

Comparing with Eq. (
78
) for the general stress tensor
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}
in the intermediate configuration,

\displaystyle\psi=\psi(0)+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon}+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}:{\boldsymbol{C}}^{\epsilon\epsilon}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon}+\boldsymbol{\omega}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}{\boldsymbol{C}}^{\boldsymbol{\omega}\epsilon}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\omega}:{\boldsymbol{C}}^{\boldsymbol{\omega}\boldsymbol{\omega}}:\boldsymbol{\omega}^{T},

(140)

we conclude that

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{C}}^{\epsilon\epsilon}=\boldsymbol{C}-p\boldsymbol{I}^{4}_{s};\hskip 23.49976pt{\boldsymbol{C}}^{\boldsymbol{\omega}\epsilon}=\mbox{\boldsymbol{$0$}};\hskip 23.49976pt{\boldsymbol{C}}^{\boldsymbol{\omega}\boldsymbol{\omega}}=-p\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}^{4}_{as};

(141)

\displaystyle{C}^{\epsilon\epsilon}_{ijkl}={C}_{ijkl}-\frac{1}{2}p\left(\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}+\delta_{il}\delta_{jk}\right);\hskip 23.49976pt{C}^{\omega\epsilon}_{ijkl}=0;\hskip 23.49976pt{C}^{\omega\omega}_{ijkl}=\frac{1}{2}p\left(\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}-\delta_{il}\delta_{jk}\right).

(142)

6.4.2
Expressions for stresses

Corresponding to Eq. (
133
) second Piola-Kirchhoff and Cauchy stresses are

\boldsymbol{T}=-p\boldsymbol{I}+\boldsymbol{C}:\boldsymbol{E};

(143)

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{\sigma}=J^{-1}(-p\boldsymbol{F}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}^{T}+\boldsymbol{F}\cdot(\boldsymbol{C}:\boldsymbol{E})\cdot\boldsymbol{F}^{T}).\end{split}

(144)

Using polar decomposition of
\boldsymbol{F}=\boldsymbol{R}\cdot\boldsymbol{U}
into the orthogonal rotation tensor
\boldsymbol{R}
and the symmetric right stretch tensor
\boldsymbol{U}
, we obtain from Eq. (
144
)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}=J^{-1}\boldsymbol{R}\cdot[-p\boldsymbol{U}\cdot\boldsymbol{U}+\boldsymbol{U}\cdot(\boldsymbol{C}:\boldsymbol{E})\cdot\boldsymbol{U}]\cdot\boldsymbol{R}^{T}=J^{-1}\boldsymbol{R}\cdot[-p(2\boldsymbol{E}+\boldsymbol{I})+\boldsymbol{U}\cdot(\boldsymbol{C}:\boldsymbol{E})\cdot\boldsymbol{U}]\cdot\boldsymbol{R}^{T}.

(145)

The linear in distortions expression for stress
\boldsymbol{T}
is obtained from Eq. (
143
):

\boldsymbol{T}=-p\boldsymbol{I}+\boldsymbol{C}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon};\hskip 23.49976ptT_{ij}=-p\delta_{ij}+{C}_{ijkl}\epsilon_{kl}.

(146)

As a general conclusion, it follows from Eqs. (
138
)
and (
146
) that
\boldsymbol{T}\neq\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}
and
\boldsymbol{C}\neq\frac{\partial^{2}\psi}{\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}
, despite that this is expected in the small strain theory. Since in many atomistic simulations elastic moduli at any pressure are determined as coefficients of the quadratic form in terms of
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
, this may lead to significant errors.
Of course, if one drops the terms
\frac{1}{2}\left(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon}+\boldsymbol{\omega}^{T}:\boldsymbol{\omega}\right)

in Eq. (
138
) for
\psi
, then
\boldsymbol{T}=\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}
and
\boldsymbol{C}=\frac{\partial^{2}\boldsymbol{T}}{\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}
hold.
However, this would be an inconsistent step, since (a) we need to keep these terms when we will derive the elastic moduli in the current configuration and (b) in atomistic calculations its contribution to the energy cannot be eliminated.

Let us elaborate Eq. (
145
) for the Cauchy stress
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
for small distortions.
In the linear approximation in distortions, we obtain

J=1+(\boldsymbol{I}:\boldsymbol{\beta})=1+\epsilon_{0};\hskip 23.49976ptJ^{-1}=1-\boldsymbol{I}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon}=1-\epsilon_{0},

(147)

since
(1+\epsilon_{0})(1-\epsilon_{0})=1-\epsilon_{0}^{2}\simeq 1
.
Utilizing Eqs. (
134
) and (
135
) in Eq. (
145
) and limiting ourselves by the linear in distortions terms in
1/J=1-\boldsymbol{I}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
,
\boldsymbol{E}=\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
and
\boldsymbol{U}=\boldsymbol{I}+\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
,
\boldsymbol{R}=\boldsymbol{I}+\boldsymbol{\omega}
(because the second-order terms will be neglected in expression for
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
), we derive:

\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(1-\boldsymbol{I}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon})(\boldsymbol{I}+\boldsymbol{\omega})\cdot[-p(\boldsymbol{I}+\boldsymbol{\epsilon})^{2}+(\boldsymbol{I}+\boldsymbol{\epsilon})\cdot(\boldsymbol{C}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon})\cdot(\boldsymbol{I}+\boldsymbol{\epsilon})]\cdot(\boldsymbol{I}+\boldsymbol{\omega}^{T}).

(148)

Then, neglecting quadratic and higher order terms in
\boldsymbol{\beta}
in Eq. (
148
), we obtain:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}=-p(\boldsymbol{I}+2\boldsymbol{\epsilon}-\boldsymbol{I}\boldsymbol{I}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon})+\boldsymbol{C}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon}=-p\boldsymbol{I}+\left(\boldsymbol{C}+p(\boldsymbol{I}\boldsymbol{I}-2\boldsymbol{I}_{s}^{4})\right):\boldsymbol{\epsilon};

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}=-p\boldsymbol{I}+\tilde{\boldsymbol{B}}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon};\hskip 23.49976pt\tilde{\boldsymbol{B}}:=\boldsymbol{C}+p(\boldsymbol{I}\boldsymbol{I}-2\boldsymbol{I}_{s}^{4}).

(149)

\displaystyle{\sigma}_{ij}=-p(\delta_{ij}+2\epsilon_{ij}-\delta_{ij}\epsilon_{mm})+{C}_{ijkl}\epsilon_{kl}=-p\delta_{ij}+\left({C}_{ijkl}+p(\delta_{ij}\delta_{kl}-\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}-\delta_{il}\delta_{jk})\right)\epsilon_{kl};

\displaystyle{\sigma}_{ij}=-p\delta_{ij}+\tilde{B}_{ijkl}\epsilon_{kl};\hskip 23.49976pt\tilde{B}_{ijkl}:={C}_{ijkl}+p(\delta_{ij}\delta_{kl}-\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}-\delta_{il}\delta_{jk}).

(150)

In the limit when the intermediate configuration coincides with the current one,
Eq. (
149
) is equivalent to the rate equation (
98
),
\boldsymbol{C}=\bar{\boldsymbol{C}}
, and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{B}}=\boldsymbol{B}
, or its integrated form Eq. (
100
). Since below we will consider small difference between the intermediate and current configurations, we will neglect differences between
\tilde{\boldsymbol{B}}
and
\boldsymbol{B}
.
While sometimes the elastic moduli
{B}_{ijkl}
are called the elastic constants
[
73
,
38
,
39
,
46
,
40
,
34
]
, they are not, because they are functions of pressure or volume.

Equations similar to Eq. (
150
) were presented in
[
74
]
with elastic moduli depending on volumetric strain instead of pressure, which are, of course, connected by an equation of state.
Explicitly Eq. (
150
) appeared in
[
29
]
and then rederived in
[
30
]
as a particular
of Eq. (
48
) for the general initial stress
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
.
Comparing Eqs. (
6
) and (
150
) we obtain

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{B}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{C}}+p(\boldsymbol{I}\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{I}^{4});\hskip 23.49976ptB_{ijkl}=\tilde{C}_{ijkl}+p(\delta_{ij}\delta_{kl}-\delta_{il}\delta_{jk}).

(151)

The tensor
\tilde{C}_{ijkl}
is not symmetric with respect to
i\leftrightarrow j
and
k\leftrightarrow l
and does not possess the Voigt symmetry, however, it is symmetric with respect to
(i,j)\leftrightarrow(k,l)
. At the same time,

B_{ijkl}
does possess the Voigt symmetry (
i\leftrightarrow j
,
k\leftrightarrow l
, and
(i,j)\leftrightarrow(k,l)
), but
is not symmetric with respect to
j\leftrightarrow l
and
i\leftrightarrow k
. Symmetrizing Eq. (
151
) with respect to
j\leftrightarrow l
, we obtain

\displaystyle\tilde{C}_{ijkl}^{s}=B_{ijkl}^{s}:=\frac{1}{2}(B_{ijkl}+B_{ilkj}).

(152)

Similar to the general case, for hydrostatically pre-stressed solid, it follows from Eqs. (
139
), (
146
),
and (
149
) that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{T}\neq\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}};\hskip 23.49976pt\boldsymbol{C}\neq\frac{\partial^{2}\psi}{\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}};\hskip 23.49976pt\boldsymbol{\sigma}\neq\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}};\hskip 23.49976pt\boldsymbol{\sigma}\neq\frac{1}{J}\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}};\hskip 23.49976pt\boldsymbol{B}\neq\frac{\partial^{2}\psi}{\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}},

(153)

despite that this is expected from small strain theory.

6.4.3
Equation of motion and wave propagation under pressure

The equations of motion (
82
) with allowing for Eq. (
152
)

\displaystyle\rho\frac{\partial^{2}u_{i}}{\partial^{2}t}=B_{ijkl}^{s}\frac{\partial^{2}u_{k}}{\partial r_{*j}\partial r_{*l}}.

(154)

The corresponding plane wave propagation equation is

\displaystyle\rho v_{w}^{2}u_{i}=B_{ijkl}^{s}k_{j}k_{l}u_{k}=L_{ik}u_{k};\hskip 23.49976ptL_{ik}:=B_{ijkl}^{s}k_{j}k_{l},

(155)

As one of the known stability conditions, the propagation matrix
L_{ik}
should be positive definite for all possible
k_{i}
to guarantee possibility of wave propagation in any direction, which shows an addition importance of the symmetrized tensors
\tilde{\boldsymbol{C}}^{s}
and tensor
\boldsymbol{B}^{s}
, which coincide when initial stress reduces to the hydrostatic pressure. Eq. (
155
) show possibility to determine
the symmetrized tensors
\tilde{\boldsymbol{C}}^{s}
and
\boldsymbol{B}^{s}
from the wave propagation experiment. The antisymmetric part of
\tilde{\boldsymbol{C}}
can be determined from the elastic energy only; the antisymmetric part of
\boldsymbol{B}
can be determined from the stress-strain relationship only.

6.4.4
Gibbs energy

First, neglecting higher than the second-degree terms in distortions
\boldsymbol{\beta}
, we obtain for the Jacobian determinant
(
[
63
]
):

J=1+\boldsymbol{I}:\boldsymbol{\beta}+\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{I}:\boldsymbol{\beta})^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\beta}:\boldsymbol{\beta}=1+\epsilon_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{0}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon}+\boldsymbol{\omega}:\boldsymbol{\omega}\right).

(156)

Let us calculate the Gibbs energy
G:=\psi+pJ
per unit volume of the intermediate configuration utilizing Eq. (
138
) for
\psi
:

\displaystyle G-\psi(0):=\psi-\psi(0)+pJ=-p(\cancel{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}:\boldsymbol{I}}+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{T}:\boldsymbol{\beta})+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}:\boldsymbol{C}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon}+

\displaystyle p[1+\cancel{\boldsymbol{I}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}+\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{I}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon})^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\beta}:\boldsymbol{\beta}]=

\displaystyle p+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}:\underbrace{\left(\boldsymbol{C}+p(\boldsymbol{I}\boldsymbol{I}-2\boldsymbol{I}_{s}^{4})\right)}_{\boldsymbol{B}}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon}=p+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}:\boldsymbol{B}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\rightarrow

\displaystyle G=\psi+pJ=\psi(0)+p+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}:\boldsymbol{B}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon},

(157)

since
\boldsymbol{\beta}:\boldsymbol{\beta}+\boldsymbol{\beta}^{T}:\boldsymbol{\beta}=2\boldsymbol{\epsilon}:\boldsymbol{\beta}=2\boldsymbol{\epsilon}:(\boldsymbol{\omega}+\boldsymbol{\epsilon})=2\boldsymbol{\epsilon}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
and
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}:\boldsymbol{\omega}=0
as the double contraction of the symmetric and antisymmetric tensors. Thus, the terms with small rotations
\boldsymbol{\omega}
in the expression (
138
) for
\psi
is compensated in and disappears from Eq. (
157
) for the Gibbs energy
G
.
This confirms that it could not be neglected in Eq. (
138
) for
\psi
.
While Gibbs energy is defined at prescribed
\theta
, in a similar way at prescribed entropy we can define the enthalpy
U+pJ
, which will possess the same expression as
G
, since we do not distinguish here between isothermal and adiabatic processes.

The most important conclusion from Eqs. (
149
) and (
157
) is that

\boldsymbol{\sigma}+p\boldsymbol{I}=\frac{\partial G}{\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}};\hskip 23.49976pt\boldsymbol{B}=\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{\sigma}}{\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}=\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}.

(158)

Thus, the effective elastic moduli in the current configuration
\boldsymbol{B}
, defined as derivative of the Cauchy stress with respect to small strain
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
superposed in the intermediate configuration at pressure
p
, can be obtained as the second derivative of the Gibbs energy per unit volume of the intermediate configuration with respect to small strains
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
. Relationship between superposed Cauchy stress
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{R}+p\boldsymbol{I}
, the Gibbs energy
G
, small strains
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
, and the effective elastic moduli in current configuration
\boldsymbol{B}
are similar to those in the traditional linear elasticity if
G

is used instead of an elastic energy. Non-traditional point in such a presentation, which was criticized in literature
[
42
]
, is that usually the Gibbs energy is expressed in terms of stresses and used to find strains. Despite this, it is strictly justified here that the Gibbs rather than the elastic energy
should be used to define the superposed Cauchy stress and the effective elastic moduli
\boldsymbol{B}
in the current configuration.
This is generally not surprising, because in contrast to classical thermodynamics, modern nonlinear continuum theory
[
72
,
29
,
30
,
31
,
63
]
, considering
thermodynamic functions with respect to arbitrary pre-stressed reference state, always mix variables, e.g., use stress
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}
(pressure
p
) and strain
\boldsymbol{E}
in elastic (internal or free) energy
\psi
in Eqs. (
6
) and (
91
). Gibbs energy in nonlinear elasticity is almost never expressed in terms of stresses, since it is practically impossible to inverse stress versus strain relationship.

Next, using Eq. (
57
) and Eq. (
158
), we obtain for bulk modulus

\displaystyle K_{V}=1/9\,\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\boldsymbol{B}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}=1/9\,\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$:$}}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}=1/9\,\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial\epsilon_{ii}\partial\epsilon_{jj}}=\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial\epsilon_{0}^{2}},

(159)

since

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\epsilon}=\frac{1}{3}\epsilon_{0}\boldsymbol{I}+\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{dev}\rightarrow\frac{d\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}{d\epsilon_{0}}=\frac{1}{3}\boldsymbol{I}\rightarrow\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial\epsilon_{0}^{2}}=\frac{d\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}{d\epsilon_{0}}:\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}:\frac{d\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}{d\epsilon_{0}}=\frac{1}{9}\boldsymbol{I}:\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}:\boldsymbol{I}.

(160)

Thus, the bulk modulus
K_{V}
is the second derivative of the Gibbs energy with respect to small volumetric strain for pure isotropic strain increment. This contrasts with the statement in
[
33
]
that the bulk modulus
K
, unlike the
\boldsymbol{B}
, is a second derivative of energy
\psi
, rather than of
G
.

6.4.5
Gibbs energy for finite strain

Let us derive expression for the Gibbs energy for finite strain
\boldsymbol{E}
in quadratic and in
\boldsymbol{E}
approximation. First,
for finite
\boldsymbol{E}
, the Jacobian determinant
(
[
63
]
, Eq. (1.8.1)):

J=\left(1+2I_{1}(\boldsymbol{E})+4I_{2}(\boldsymbol{E})+8I_{3}(\boldsymbol{E})\right)^{1/2},

(161)

where
I_{i}(\boldsymbol{E})
are invariants of
\boldsymbol{E}
:

\displaystyle I_{1}(\boldsymbol{E})=\boldsymbol{I}:\boldsymbol{E}=E_{ii};\hskip 23.49976ptI_{2}(\boldsymbol{E})=\frac{1}{2}((\boldsymbol{I}:\boldsymbol{E})^{2}-\boldsymbol{E}:\boldsymbol{E})=\frac{1}{2}((E_{ii})^{2}-E_{ij}E_{ij});

\displaystyle I_{3}(\boldsymbol{E})=\frac{1}{6}\left((\boldsymbol{I}:\boldsymbol{E})^{3}-3(\boldsymbol{I}:\boldsymbol{E})\boldsymbol{E}:\boldsymbol{E}+2\boldsymbol{E}:\boldsymbol{E}\cdot\boldsymbol{E}\right)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{6}\left((E_{ii})^{3}-3E_{ii}E_{ij}E_{ij}+2E_{ij}E_{jk}E_{ki}\right).

(162)

For quadratic in
\boldsymbol{E}
approximation
I_{3}
can be neglected and

\displaystyle J\simeq 1+I_{1}(\boldsymbol{E})+2I_{2}(\boldsymbol{E})-\frac{1}{2}(I_{1}(\boldsymbol{E})+\cancel{2I_{2}(\boldsymbol{E})})^{2}\simeq

\displaystyle 1+\boldsymbol{I}:\boldsymbol{E}+\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{I}:\boldsymbol{E})^{2}-\boldsymbol{E}:\boldsymbol{E}=1+E_{ii}+\frac{1}{2}(E_{ii})^{2}-E_{ij}E_{ij}.

(163)

Let us calculate the Gibbs energy
G:=\psi+pJ
per unit volume of the intermediate configuration utilizing Eq. (
133
) for
\psi
:

\displaystyle G-\psi(0):=\psi-\psi(0)+pJ=\cancel{-p\boldsymbol{I}:\boldsymbol{E}}+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{E}:\boldsymbol{C}:\boldsymbol{E}+

\displaystyle p[1+\cancel{\boldsymbol{I}:\boldsymbol{E}}+\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{I}:\boldsymbol{E})^{2}-\boldsymbol{E}:\boldsymbol{E}]=

\displaystyle p+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{E}:\underbrace{\left(\boldsymbol{C}+p(\boldsymbol{I}\boldsymbol{I}-2\boldsymbol{I}_{s}^{4})\right)}_{\boldsymbol{B}}:\boldsymbol{E}=p+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{E}:\boldsymbol{B}:\boldsymbol{E}\rightarrow

\displaystyle G=\psi+pJ=\psi(0)+p+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{E}:\boldsymbol{B}:\boldsymbol{E},

(164)

i.e.,
G
has the same expression for finite
\boldsymbol{E}
as for small strains, involving elasticity moduli
\boldsymbol{B}
.
The function
U+p(J-1)
close to the enthalpy, similar to the Gibbs energy, was defined for finite strain in
[
32
]

up to fourth-degree in
\boldsymbol{E}
approximation, and explicit expressions for
\boldsymbol{B}
and higher-order elastic moduli were given for cubic crystals.
However, practical application of Eq. (
164
) for determination of elastic moduli (this was the main goal in
[
32
]
) even up to second-degree in
\boldsymbol{E}
approximation is limited to the small strain.
First, since in atomistic simulations one can find
\boldsymbol{B}
from
G
for small
\boldsymbol{E}\simeq\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
, then why one would do this for finite strain? Second, in
[
32
]
and following papers
[
37
,
35
,
36
]
, relation between
G
and stress
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
was not given. In fact,
for finite components of
\boldsymbol{E}
not negligible in comparison with
1
,

\boldsymbol{\sigma}+p\boldsymbol{I}\neq\frac{\partial G}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}}=\boldsymbol{B}:\boldsymbol{E},

(165)

because for linear
\boldsymbol{T}-\boldsymbol{E}
relationship
\boldsymbol{\sigma}-\boldsymbol{E}
relationship contains non-negligible nonlinear terms.

6.4.6
Elastic energy for isochoric deformation with respect to the intermediate configuration

For this particular case,
J=det(\boldsymbol{I}+\boldsymbol{\beta})=1
, and it follows from Eq. (
157
)

\displaystyle\psi_{j=1}-\psi(0):=G_{j=1}-\psi(0)-p=\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{j=1}:\boldsymbol{B}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{j=1},

(166)

where subscript
j=1
means that parameter is determined at
J=1
.

Let us consider examples of one-parametric isochoric distortions. One of examples is simple shear
\boldsymbol{F}=\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}+\gamma\boldsymbol{m}\boldsymbol{n}
. However, rotation-free shear,
\boldsymbol{F}=\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}+\frac{1}{2}\gamma(\boldsymbol{m}\boldsymbol{n}+\boldsymbol{n}\boldsymbol{m})
,
\boldsymbol{\omega}=\mbox{\boldsymbol{$0$}}
, is not isochoric because
det(\boldsymbol{F})=1-\frac{1}{4}\gamma^{2}
. The straightforward way to correct this is to use the tensor
\boldsymbol{F}_{is}=J(\boldsymbol{F})^{-1/3}\boldsymbol{F}
:
det(\boldsymbol{F}_{is})=J(\boldsymbol{F})^{-1}det(\boldsymbol{F})=1
.
In particular, for the rotation-free shear,

\boldsymbol{F}=(1-\frac{1}{4}\gamma^{2})^{-1/3}(\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}+\frac{1}{2}\gamma(\boldsymbol{m}\boldsymbol{n}+\boldsymbol{n}\boldsymbol{m}))
, then

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\epsilon}=\boldsymbol{F}-\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}=\boldsymbol{\beta}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{(1-\frac{1}{4}\gamma^{2})^{1/3}}-1&\frac{\frac{1}{2}\gamma}{(1-\frac{1}{4}\gamma^{2})^{1/3}}&0\\
\frac{\frac{1}{2}\gamma}{(1-\frac{1}{4}\gamma^{2})^{1/3}}&\frac{1}{(1-\frac{1}{4}\gamma^{2})^{1/3}}-1&0\\
0&0&\frac{1}{(1-\frac{1}{4}\gamma^{2})^{1/3}}-1\\
\end{pmatrix}\simeq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\gamma^{2}}{6}&\gamma&0\\
\gamma&\frac{\gamma^{2}}{6}&0\\
0&0&\frac{\gamma^{2}}{6}\\
\end{pmatrix}+O(\gamma^{4});\hskip 23.49976ptdet(\boldsymbol{I}+\boldsymbol{\epsilon})=1+\frac{\gamma^{6}}{1728}\simeq 1.

(167)

Simpler option is to use expression for finite strain
\boldsymbol{E}
for simple shear from Eq. (
84
)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\epsilon}=\boldsymbol{\beta}=\frac{1}{2}\gamma(\boldsymbol{m}\boldsymbol{n}+\boldsymbol{n}\boldsymbol{m}+\frac{1}{2}\gamma\boldsymbol{n}\boldsymbol{n})=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}0&\gamma&0\\
\gamma&\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{2}&0\\
0&0&0\\
\end{pmatrix};\hskip 23.49976ptdet(\boldsymbol{I}+\boldsymbol{\epsilon})=1.

(168)

This and other correction options for different strain states can be obtained by placing unknown
x
in some positions and determining
it from
J=1
or
J=1+O(x^{3})
. Important point here is that some components of the strain tensor are of higher order of smallness. They should be kept when elastic energy is evaluated in atomistic simulations. However, when substituted in
Eq. (
166
), the second order terms can be neglected because they produce higher than second-order terms in expression for energy. In particular, for strain Eq. (
168
),
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}=\frac{1}{2}\gamma(\boldsymbol{m}\boldsymbol{n}+\boldsymbol{n}\boldsymbol{m})

contributes to the energy. Then, when neglecting second-order terms in distortions, incompressibility constraint in Eq. (
156
) reduces to
\epsilon_{0}=0
. Decomposing small strain

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\epsilon}=\frac{1}{3}\epsilon_{0}\boldsymbol{I}+\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{dev}

(169)

into spherical and deviatoric parts, Eq. (
166
) specifies to

\displaystyle\psi_{j=1}-\psi(0)=\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{dev}:\boldsymbol{B}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{dev}.

(170)

It is clear that not all components of
\boldsymbol{B}
contribute to the energy and can be found by calculating
\psi_{j=1}
. It was mentioned in
[
37
]
that shear moduli only can be determined, but as we will show explicitly, not only. To find explicit expression for the contributing tensor, we utilize the volumetric
(
\;{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$V$}}}\,=\,1/3\;{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}}\,{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}}\;
) and deviatoric

\,\left({\mbox{\boldsymbol{$D$}}}\right)\,
parts of the symmetrizing fourth-order unit tensor

\;{\boldsymbol{I}^{4}_{s}}\,=\,{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$V$}}}+{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$D$}}}\;
,

\,V_{ijkl}\,=\,1/3\,\delta_{ij}\,\delta_{kl}\,
,

\,D_{ijkl}\,=\,1/2\,\left(\delta_{ik}\,\delta_{jl}+\delta_{il}\,\delta_{jk}-2/3\,\delta_{ij}\,\delta_{kl}\right)\,
. The tensors

V
and

D
possess the following properties:

\,{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$V$}}}:{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$V$}}}\,=\,{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$V$}}}\,
;

\,{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$D$}}}:{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$D$}}}\,=\,{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$D$}}}\,
;

\,{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$V$}}}:{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$D$}}}\,=\,{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$D$}}}:{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$V$}}}\,=\,0\,
;

\,{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$V$}}}:{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$A$}}}\,=\,1/3\left({\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}}:{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$A$}}}\right){\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}}\,
;

\,{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$D$}}}:{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$A$}}}\,=\,{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$A$}}}_{dev}\,
;

\,{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$A$}}}\,=\,{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$V$}}}:{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$A$}}}+{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$D$}}}:{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$A$}}}\,
,

i.e., they allow one to separate the volumetric and deviatoric parts of a symmetric tensor
A
.
Matrix presentation of tensors
\boldsymbol{I}^{4}
,
\boldsymbol{I}^{4}_{s}
,
\boldsymbol{I}^{4}_{as}
,
V
, and
D
is given in Appendix B.
Then we can present Eq. (
170
) as

\displaystyle\psi_{j=1}-\psi(0)=\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}:\mbox{\boldsymbol{$D$}}:{\boldsymbol{B}}:\mbox{\boldsymbol{$D$}}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon}=\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}:\boldsymbol{B}_{j=1}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon};\hskip 23.49976pt\boldsymbol{B}_{j=1}:=\mbox{\boldsymbol{$D$}}:{\boldsymbol{B}}:\mbox{\boldsymbol{$D$}},

(171)

i.e.,
\psi_{j=1}
depends on the deviatoric projection
\boldsymbol{B}_{j=1}
of the tensor
\boldsymbol{B}
. In the explicit form,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{B}_{j=1}=\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{B}_{1-3}&\boldsymbol{B}_{1-6}\\
\boldsymbol{B}_{1-6}^{T}&\boldsymbol{B}_{4-6}\\
\end{pmatrix};\hskip 23.49976pt\boldsymbol{B}_{4-6}=\begin{pmatrix}B_{44}&B_{45}&B_{46}\\
B_{45}&B_{55}&B_{56}\\
B_{46}&B_{56}&B_{66}\\
\end{pmatrix},

(172)

with

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{B}_{1-3}=\frac{1}{9}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{B}_{11}&\hat{B}_{12}&\hat{B}_{13}\\
\hat{B}_{12}&B_{22}&\hat{B}_{23}\\
\hat{B}_{13}&\hat{B}_{23}&\hat{B}_{33}\\
\end{pmatrix};

(173)

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{11}=4{B_{11}}-4{B_{12}}-4{B_{13}}+{B_{22}}+2{B_{23}}+{B_{33}};

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{12}=-2{B_{11}}+5{B_{12}}-{B_{13}}-2{B_{22}}-{B_{23}}+{B_{33}};\hskip 11.74988pt

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{13}=-2{B_{11}}-{B_{12}}+5{B_{13}}+{B_{22}}-{B_{23}}-2{B_{33}};\hskip 11.74988pt

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{22}={B_{11}}-4{B_{12}}+2{B_{13}}+4{B_{22}}-4{B_{23}}+{B_{33}};

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{23}={B_{11}}-{B_{12}}-{B_{13}}-2{B_{22}}+5{B_{23}}-2{B_{33}};\hskip 11.74988pt

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{33}={B_{11}}+2{B_{12}}-4{B_{13}}+{B_{22}}-4{B_{23}}+4{B_{33}};

(174)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{B}_{1-6}=\begin{pmatrix}\hat{B}_{14}&\hat{B}_{15}&\hat{B}_{16}\\
\hat{B}_{24}&\hat{B}_{25}&\hat{B}_{26}\\
\hat{B}_{34}&\hat{B}_{35}&\hat{B}_{36}\\
\end{pmatrix}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}2B_{14}-B_{24}-B_{34}&2B_{15}-B_{25}-B_{35}&2B_{16}-B_{26}-B_{36}\\
-B_{14}+2B_{24}-B_{34}&-B_{15}+2B_{25}-B_{35}&-B_{16}+2B_{26}-B_{36}\\
-B_{14}-B_{24}+2B_{34}&-B_{15}-B_{25}+2B_{35}&-B_{16}-B_{26}+2B_{36}\\
\end{array}\right).

Matrix
\boldsymbol{B}_{j=1}
has the block structure: the
3\times 3
upper left block
\boldsymbol{B}_{1-3}
contains all
B_{ij}
for
i,j=1,\,2,\,3
(which participate in the definition of the bulk modulus
K
);
the
6\times 6
upper right block
\boldsymbol{B}_{1-6}
contains all
B_{ij}
for
i=1,\,2,\,3
and
j=4,\,5,\,6
;
and the
3\times 3
lower right block
\boldsymbol{B}_{4-6}
contains all
B_{ij}
for
i,j=4,\,5,\,6
. Evidently,
\boldsymbol{B}_{4-6}
is not affected by the deviatoric projection and can be found by determining energy under different combinations of all shear strains supplemented by second-order normal strains making them isochoric.

This is a very important result that for isochoric deformation and up to the second order in distortion
\boldsymbol{\beta}
approximation,
the elastic energy
\psi_{j=1}-\psi(0)
differs from the Gibbs potential
G_{j=1}-\psi(0)
by
\boldsymbol{\beta}
-independent term
p
only and it represents quadratic form in small strains with the effective elastic moduli
\boldsymbol{B}_{j=1}
.
Then the effective elastic moduli
\boldsymbol{B}_{j=1}
can be determined
from the atomistic simulations, by calculating the elastic energy
\psi_{j=1}-\psi(0)
for different volume-preserving distortions
\boldsymbol{\beta}
and then approximating results by quadratic function Eq. (
81
).

The question arises: since
\boldsymbol{B}_{j=1}
does not include all components of the tensor
\boldsymbol{B}
, how then to determine the remaining components with using Eq. (
157
) for Gibbs energy.
The answer is straightforward: they can be found from the consistency conditions for
\boldsymbol{B}
introduced in Section
6.2
. Let us do some accounting while using Voigt presentation of the tensors
\boldsymbol{B}
and
\boldsymbol{B}_{j=1}
as symmetric
6\times 6
matrices. For the lowest (triclinic) symmetry, matrix
\boldsymbol{B}
has total
36
components, including
6
diagonal and
30/2=15
independent off-diagonal components, i.e., total
21
independent components. Since
\boldsymbol{B}_{j=1}
is contracted from both sides by
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{dev}
with
5
independent components, it is equivalent to
5\times 5
matrix,
25
components, including
5
diagonal and
20/2=10
independent off-diagonal components, i.e., total
15
independent components.

Thus, one needs
15
calculations for
15
independent isochoric distortions to find equations for
15
components of
\boldsymbol{B}_{j=1}
and consequently
\boldsymbol{B}
. Missing
6
components of the
\boldsymbol{B}
tensor can be found from
6
consistency conditions (
113
) that come from measurement of
6
components of deformation gradient

\boldsymbol{F}_{*}(p)
in Eq. (
89
). This is illustrated in more detail in Appendix B.

For higher symmetry, there are less missing component of the tensor
\boldsymbol{B}
and less components of deformation gradient

\boldsymbol{F}_{*}(p)
in Eq. (
89
). For example, for orthorhombic, tetragonal (classes
4mm
,
\bar{4}2m
,
42m
, and
4/mmm
), hexagonal, and cubic lattices
\boldsymbol{B}_{1-6}=\mbox{\boldsymbol{$0$}}
, i.e., 3 missing equations disappear.
For monoclinic and orthorhombic lattices, 3 missing equations for matrix
\boldsymbol{B}_{1-3}
can be substituted with 3 equations for diagonal deformation gradient
\boldsymbol{F}_{*}(p)
. For trigonal, tetragonal, and hexagonal lattices, two equations for diagonal deformation gradient
\boldsymbol{F}_{*}(p)
supplement two independent equations from the system Eqs. (
9
) for strains different from
a\boldsymbol{F}_{*}(p)
for arbitrary
a
.
For an isotropic material,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{B}=3K\mbox{\boldsymbol{$V$}}+2\mu\mbox{\boldsymbol{$D$}}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{B}_{j=1}=2\mu\mbox{\boldsymbol{$D$}},

(179)

where
\mu
is the shear modulus, and we took into account that
\mbox{\boldsymbol{$V$}}:\mbox{\boldsymbol{$D$}}=\mbox{\boldsymbol{$0$}}
. For cubic and isotropic materials, one consistency condition, e.g., for bulk moduli, substitutes one missing equation.
Next, we can present
Eq. (
157
) for the Gibbs energy as

\displaystyle G-\psi(0)-p=\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}:\boldsymbol{B}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon}=\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}:\boldsymbol{I}^{4}_{s}:\boldsymbol{B}:\boldsymbol{I}^{4}_{s}:\boldsymbol{\epsilon}=\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}:({\mbox{\boldsymbol{$V$}}}+{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$D$}}}):\boldsymbol{B}:({\mbox{\boldsymbol{$V$}}}+{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$D$}}}):\boldsymbol{\epsilon}=

(180)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}:({\mbox{\boldsymbol{$V$}}}:\boldsymbol{B}:{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$V$}}}+{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$D$}}}:\boldsymbol{B}:{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$D$}}}+2{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$V$}}}:\boldsymbol{B}:{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$D$}}}):\boldsymbol{\epsilon}=\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}:(K\boldsymbol{I}\boldsymbol{I}+\frac{2}{3}\boldsymbol{I}\boldsymbol{I}:\boldsymbol{B}:{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$D$}}}):\boldsymbol{\epsilon}+\psi_{j=1}-\psi(0),

where we took into account
\frac{1}{9}\boldsymbol{I}\boldsymbol{I}:\boldsymbol{B}:\boldsymbol{I}\boldsymbol{I}=K\boldsymbol{I}\boldsymbol{I}
(see definition of
K
in Eq. (
57
)).

Also, Eq. (
158
) can be elaborated as

\boldsymbol{\sigma}+p\boldsymbol{I}=\frac{\partial G}{\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}=\frac{\partial G}{\partial\epsilon_{0}}\boldsymbol{I}+\frac{\partial G}{\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{dev}}=\frac{\partial G}{\partial\epsilon_{0}}\boldsymbol{I}+\frac{\partial G-\psi_{j=1}}{\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{dev}}+\frac{\partial\psi_{j=1}}{\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{dev}}\neq\frac{\partial G}{\partial\epsilon_{0}}\boldsymbol{I}+\frac{\partial\psi_{j=1}}{\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{dev}}

(181)

because of cross
{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$V$}}}-{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$D$}}}
term in Eq. (
180
), i.e., there is no sense to use
\psi_{j=1}
for presentation of stresses. However, based on Eq. (
171
)

{\boldsymbol{B}}_{j=1}=\frac{\partial^{2}\psi_{j=1}}{\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\partial\boldsymbol{\epsilon}},

(182)

which is part of Eq. (
158
).

6.4.7
Hydrostatic loading and isotropic deformation through energy minimization

In many papers, e.g.,
[
44
,
38
,
39
]
for tetragonal and hexagonal crystals, energy is calculated as function of volume for parameter
c/a
that minimizes an energy. But they do not describe what does this minimization mean in terms of stress state. Here, we will show for an arbitrary crystal, that energy minimization at fixed volume leads either to pure hydrostatic stress state or to isotropic deformation with respect to the current state. The power balance is

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}:\boldsymbol{d}dt=-pd{\varepsilon}_{0}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{dev}:\boldsymbol{d}_{dev}dt=J^{-1}d\psi,

(183)

where we considered Eq. (
54
). For fixed volume one has
d\varepsilon_{0}=0
. If energy is minimized with respect to all strains (or lattice parameters) at fixed volume, then
d\psi=0
and Eq. (
183
) can be satisfied at
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{dev}=\mbox{\boldsymbol{$0$}}
or
\boldsymbol{d}_{dev}=\mbox{\boldsymbol{$0$}}
only.

It is stated in
[
33
]
that the equilibrium
c/a
at prescribed
p
does not correspond to the energy minimum. However, as it was just proven,
c/a
corresponding to the pure hydrostatic loading does minimize energy at fixed volume
d\varepsilon_{0}=0
. It is also stated in
[
33
]
that the equilibrium
c/a
at prescribed
p
corresponds to the Gibbs energy minimum. This can be proven in the general case. Substituting
\psi=G-pJ
in Eq. (
183
), we obtain

\displaystyle-pJ^{-1}dJ+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{dev}:\boldsymbol{d}_{dev}dt=J^{-1}(dG-pdJ-Jdp)\hskip 11.74988pt\rightarrow\hskip 11.74988pt\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{dev}:\boldsymbol{d}_{dev}dt+dp=J^{-1}dG.

(184)

Thus, for constant
p
and strains minimizing
G
, i.e., for
dp=dG=0
, Eq. (
184
) results in
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{dev}=0
or
\boldsymbol{d}_{dev}=\mbox{\boldsymbol{$0$}}
, i.e., either in hydrostatic loading or isotropic deformation.

6.5
Simplification for isotropic materials and cubic crystals

For isotropic materials and cubic crystals, the intermediate configuration under hydrostatic pressure is produced by
isotropic deformation
. Then Eqs. (
1
) and (
4
) simplify to

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{F}_{*}=a\boldsymbol{I}\;\rightarrow\hskip 11.74988pt\boldsymbol{F}_{0}=a\boldsymbol{F}\hskip 23.49976ptJ_{*}=a^{3};\hskip 23.49976ptJ_{0}=a^{3}J;\hskip 11.74988pt\boldsymbol{E}_{*}=\frac{1}{2}(a^{2}-1)\boldsymbol{I};

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{E}_{0}=a^{2}\boldsymbol{E}+\frac{1}{2}(a^{2}-1)\boldsymbol{I};\;\rightarrow\;\boldsymbol{E}=(\boldsymbol{E}_{0}-\frac{1}{2}(a^{2}-1)\boldsymbol{I})/a^{2}.

(185)

To derive relationships between
\boldsymbol{C}
and
\boldsymbol{C}_{0}
for isotropic materials and cubic crystals, we evaluate using Eq. (
185
)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}}=a^{2}\mbox{\boldsymbol{$I$}}^{4}_{s};\hskip 23.49976pt\frac{\partial E_{0ij}}{\partial E_{kl}}=a^{2}I^{4}_{s,ijkl}=\frac{1}{2}a^{2}\left(\delta_{ki}\delta_{lj}+\delta_{li}\delta_{kj}\right).

(186)

These equations are a particular case of Eq. (
17
).
Next, using Eqs. (
8
) and (
185
), we obtain for the second Piola-Kirchhoff stress in the intermediate configuration:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{T}=\frac{1}{a}\boldsymbol{T}_{0}=\frac{1}{a}\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial\boldsymbol{E}_{0}};\hskip 23.49976ptT_{ij}=\frac{1}{a}T_{0,ij}=\frac{1}{a}\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial E_{0,ij}}.

(187)

Based on Eq. (
21
), relationship between elastic moduli in the intermediate and stress-free configurations
reduces to

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{C}(\boldsymbol{E})=a\boldsymbol{C}_{0}(\boldsymbol{E}_{0});\hskip 11.74988ptC_{ijkl}(E_{mn})=aC_{0,ijkl}(E_{0,mn}).

(188)

Eqs. (
26
) and (
27
) for
\boldsymbol{E}_{0}=\boldsymbol{E}_{*}
simplify to

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{C}_{0}\left(\boldsymbol{E}_{*}\right)=\boldsymbol{C}_{0}^{4}+\frac{1}{2}(a^{2}-1)\boldsymbol{C}_{0}^{6}:\mbox{\boldsymbol{$\boldsymbol{I}$}}+\frac{1}{8}(a^{2}-1)^{2}(\boldsymbol{C}_{0}^{8}:\mbox{\boldsymbol{$\boldsymbol{I}$}}):\mbox{\boldsymbol{$\boldsymbol{I}$}}+

\displaystyle 1/(3!)\frac{1}{8}(a^{2}-1)^{3}((\boldsymbol{C}_{0}^{10}:\mbox{\boldsymbol{$\boldsymbol{I}$}}):\mbox{\boldsymbol{$\boldsymbol{I}$}}):\mbox{\boldsymbol{$\boldsymbol{I}$}}+...\,;

(189)

\displaystyle C_{0,ijkl}\left(E_{*ab}\right)=C_{0,ijkl}^{4}+\frac{1}{2}(a^{2}-1)C_{0,ijklff}^{6}+\frac{1}{8}(a^{2}-1)^{2}C_{0,ijklffss}^{8}+

\displaystyle 1/(3!)\frac{1}{8}(a^{2}-1)^{3}C_{0,ijklffssmm}^{10}+...\,.

(190)

Eq. (
94
) with the help of Eq. (
185
) reduces to the traditional equation of state

\displaystyle-p\boldsymbol{I}=\frac{1}{a}\frac{\partial{\psi_{0}}}{\partial{\boldsymbol{E}_{0}}}\mid_{\boldsymbol{E}_{0}=\frac{1}{2}(a^{2}-1)\boldsymbol{I}};\hskip 23.49976pt-p\delta_{ij}=\frac{1}{a}\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial E_{0,kl}}\mid_{E_{0,ab}=\frac{1}{2}(a^{2}-1)\delta_{ab}}\,.

(191)

Consistency condition (
108
) for elastic compliances reduces to

\boldsymbol{\lambda}(p):\boldsymbol{I}=-\frac{1}{a}\frac{da(p)}{dp}\boldsymbol{I}=-\frac{d\ln a(p)}{dp}\boldsymbol{I}\hskip 11.74988pt\rightarrow\hskip 11.74988pt\lambda_{ijkk}=-\frac{1}{a}\frac{da(p)}{dp}\delta_{ij}=-\frac{d\ln a(p)}{dp}\delta_{ij}.

(192)

Since for cubic crystals
\lambda_{11kk}=\lambda_{22kk}=\lambda_{33kk}
and
\lambda_{1122}=\lambda_{1133}
, then Eq. (
192
)
specifies to

\lambda_{1111}+2\lambda_{1122}=-\frac{1}{a}\frac{da(p)}{dp}=-\frac{d\ln a(p)}{dp}.

(193)

For cubic system, due to symmetry, bulk compliance is

k=\lambda_{iikk}=3(\lambda_{1111}+2\lambda_{1122}).

(194)

Then Eq. (
193
) can be transformed to

k=-3\frac{d\ln a(p)}{dp}=-\frac{d\ln a^{3}(p)}{dp}=-\frac{d\ln J(p)}{dp},

(195)

i.e., it coincides with constraint related to the bulk compliance.
We eliminated subscript
R
for
k
, because for cubic system Reuss and Voigt approximations for bulk modulus/compressibility coincide.

Consistency condition (
113
) for elastic moduli
\boldsymbol{B}
reduces to

\displaystyle-\dot{p}\boldsymbol{I}=-\boldsymbol{B}(p):\boldsymbol{I}\frac{1}{a}\frac{da(p)}{dp}=-\boldsymbol{B}(p):\boldsymbol{I}\frac{d\ln a(p)}{dp}\hskip 11.74988pt\rightarrow\hskip 11.74988pt

\displaystyle\delta_{ij}=-B_{ijkk}\frac{1}{a}\frac{dp}{da}=-B_{ijkk}\frac{dp}{d\ln a}.

(196)

Since for cubic crystals
B_{11kk}=B_{22kk}=B_{33kk}
and
B_{1122}=B_{1133}
, then Eq. (
196
)
specifies to

\displaystyle B_{1111}+2B_{1122}=\frac{dp}{d\ln a}.

(197)

For cubic system, due to symmetry, the bulk modulus is

K=\frac{1}{9}B_{iikk}=\frac{1}{3}(B_{1111}+2B_{1122}).

(198)

Then Eq. (
197
) can be transformed to

\displaystyle K=\frac{1}{3}\frac{dp}{d\ln a}=\frac{dp}{d\ln a^{3}}=\frac{dp}{d\ln J},

(199)

i.e., it coincides with constraint related to the bulk modulus.

7
THE PRINCIPLE OF SUPERPOSITION FOR DEFECTS AND INELASTICITY IN NONLINEAR ELASTICITY

One of the particular cases of application of Eq. (
47
) (or Eq. (
101
)) is when stress
\boldsymbol{\sigma}(t)
(pressure
p
) is homogeneous (i.e., satisfies the equilibrium equation) and there is a relatively small perturbation of strains that can be described within small strain approximation. Then all nonlinearities are included in a homogeneous solution, which automatically satisfies the equilibrium equations.
Then the boundary-value problem should be solved for small-strain linear elastic constitutive relationships between increments of stress
\Delta\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}(t+\Delta t)-\boldsymbol{\sigma}(t)
and strain
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
and rotations
\boldsymbol{\omega}
. If, as it is done in traditional linear elasticity, the effect of small rotations is neglected, then the constitutive
equations for the linearized incremental problem coincide with those for linear elasticity. In particular, one can apply
known analytical solutions for
defects (e.g., cracks, point defects, dislocations, disclinations, inclusions of different phases and twins) and their evolution by just changing traditional elastic moduli
\boldsymbol{C}_{0}
with stress-dependent elastic moduli
\boldsymbol{B}
. Eshelby solution for ellipsoidal inclusion is another example, which is broadly used for the description of phase transformations,
twinning, cracks, and effective elastic properties of polycrystalline and multiphase materials.

The principle of superposition can be used while combining different dislocations, inclusions, grains, etc.
Also, the small-strain incremental inelastic problem can be included in a standard way similar to the linear elastic material,
like elastoplasticity, creep, phase transformation, damage, etc.

One of the possible limitations on stress
\boldsymbol{\sigma}(t)
for analytical solutions is that it should preserve the full Voigt symmetry, like in linear elasticity, because analytical solutions were not found for
B_{ijkl}\neq B_{klij}
.
In addition, the non-existence of analytical solutions for low-symmetry lattices also limits the type of applied stress
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
.

As an example, let consider a homogenous isotropic body under pressure
p
as the intermediate reference configuration. Utilizing dislocation theory within linear elasticity approximation,
the normal and shear stress fields of the
superdislocation (which could also approximate a dislocation pile-up) in the polar coordinate
r,\,\phi
are
[
75
]
:

\displaystyle\sigma_{rr}=\sigma_{\phi\phi}=-p+\frac{\mu_{*}N|{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$b$}}_{*}}|}{2\left(\pi\left(1-\nu(p)\right)\right)}\frac{\sin\phi}{r}=-p+\frac{\mu(p)(3K(p)+2\mu(p))N|{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$b$}}_{*}}|}{\left(\pi\left(3K(p)+4\mu(p)\right)\right)}\frac{\sin\phi}{r};

\displaystyle\tau=\frac{\mu(p)N|{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$b$}}_{*}}|}{2\left(\pi\left(1-\nu(p)\right)\right)}\frac{\cos\phi}{r}=\frac{\mu(p)(3K(p)+2\mu(p))N|{\mbox{\boldsymbol{$b$}}_{*}}|}{\left(\pi\left(3K(p)+4\mu(p)\right)\right)}\frac{\cos\phi}{r},

(200)

where
\mbox{\boldsymbol{$b$}}_{*}
is the Burgers vector in the intermediate configuration under pressure
p
,
N
is the number if dislocations, and
\nu
is the Poison ratio. Generally,
\mbox{\boldsymbol{$b$}}_{*}=\boldsymbol{F}_{*}\cdot\mbox{\boldsymbol{$b$}}
, but for isotropic material
\mbox{\boldsymbol{$b$}}_{*}=a\mbox{\boldsymbol{$b$}}
.
Pressure-dependent moduli
K(p)
and
\mu(p)
can be found in terms of
\boldsymbol{B}(p)
using equations for the effective elastic properties
(
129
) and (
131
) for crystals of arbitrary symmetry.
Similarly, these expressions can be generalized for single crystals, using analytical solutions for anisotropic elasticity from
[
75
]
and can be used to determine the interaction between dislocations, dislocation and other defects and inclusions.

As an example, let us qualitatively analyze the effect of dislocation pileup on the drastic reduction of phase transformation pressure due to
large plastic shear. For example, pressure reduced from 52.5 to 6.7 GPa for transformation from hexagonal nanocrystalline BN to superhard wurtzitic BN
[
76
]
and from 70 to 0.7 GPa for transformation from graphite to cubic diamond
[
77
]
; see also review
[
78
]
for other examples. As the main hypothesis for explaining such a transformation pressure reduction, strong stress concentration at the tip of the plastic-strain-induced dislocation pileups is considered.
Both normal and shear stresses at the tip of the pileup significantly contribute to the total stresses and reach the lattice instability
criterion under complex loading (see
[
13
,
14
,
15
]
)
and produce local nucleation of the high-pressure phase.
A simple analytical model in
[
79
,
80
]
based on Eq. (
200
) but with pressure-independent elastic moduli was
used for analytical estimates, which in fact correspond well to more complex phase-field modeling
[
81
,
82
,
83
]
, which also did not include pressure dependence of the elastic moduli.

Let us demonstrate that allowing for pressure dependence of the elastic moduli in Eq. (
200
) can lead to some useful qualitative analysis. First, due to
\mbox{\boldsymbol{$b$}}_{*}=a\mbox{\boldsymbol{$b$}}
, stress concentration under pressure reduces by a factor of
a
. Then, phase transformation starts with crystal lattice instability at the dislocation pileup, either due to elastic instability or phonon instability. For
elastic shear instability,
\mu(p)\rightarrow 0
, and the effect of stress concentrations is getting small. In contrast, for phonon instabilities, shear modulus should not reduce and may even grow; thus, promoting such transformations by dislocation pileups will be much more effective. In more detail, for shear instability, if applied pressure
p
is much below the instability pressure,
\mu
can be large enough, and the heterogeneous pressure distribution due to pileup with large
N
can cause local instabilities and phase transformations. In this case, the principle of superposition is, of course, a big assumption, which still could work beyond some distance from the tip. Since screw dislocation does not generate pressure, its contribution to lattice instability is much lower even for constant
\mu
. Then transformation may start quite close to pressure, for which
\mu(p)=0
, which makes the concentration of shear stresses small as well. This approach can be applied for single crystals, using equations for dislocations in anisotropic elasticity presented in
[
75
]
.

8
SUMMARY

In the paper, a general nonlinear theory for the elasticity of pre-stressed single crystals is systematically presented. Various types of elastic moduli are defined, their importance is determined, and relationships between them are presented.

8.1
Pre-stressing with arbitrary stress
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{*}

Complete information about the nonlinear elasticity of a crystal under arbitrary pressure is contained in the Taylor expansion of the elastic energy Eq. (
22
) to a high enough degree with respect to the stress-free state. This expansion defines

n^{th}
-rank elastic constants
\boldsymbol{C}_{0}^{n}
, which possess symmetry of the undeformed crystal. These are the only existing elastic constants; all other elastic moduli are functions of initial stress or pressure,
\boldsymbol{E}_{0}
, or
\boldsymbol{E}
, and the deformation gradient
\boldsymbol{F}_{*}
.
They have symmetry with respect to exchanging all pairs of indices and within each pair.

The second-order elastic moduli in the reference
\boldsymbol{C}_{0}\left(\boldsymbol{E}_{0}\right)
and intermediate
\boldsymbol{C}\left(\boldsymbol{E}\right)
configurations are defined in a natural way in Eq. (
12
) via the first or second derivatives of the second Piola-Kirchhoff stress or elastic energy, respectively, with respect to Lagrangian strains in corresponding configurations. They also can be found from the linear relationship (
32
)
\dot{\boldsymbol{T}}=\boldsymbol{C}:\dot{\boldsymbol{E}}
. They represent tangent moduli in stress-strain
\boldsymbol{T}_{0}-\boldsymbol{E}_{0}
or
\boldsymbol{T}-\boldsymbol{E}
curves at any chosen strain
\boldsymbol{E}_{0}
or
\boldsymbol{E}_{0}
.
Moduli
\boldsymbol{C}_{0}\left(\boldsymbol{E}_{0}\right)
and
\boldsymbol{C}\left(\boldsymbol{E}\right)
do not keep symmetry of the initial nondeformed lattice and for general
\boldsymbol{E}_{0}
or
\boldsymbol{E}
have symmetry of a trigonal crystal, i.e., all 21 unequal components. They have complete Voigt symmetry and
are related to each other by Eq. (
21
). Also,
\boldsymbol{C}_{0}\left(\boldsymbol{E}_{0}\right)
is related to elastic constants
\boldsymbol{C}_{0}^{n}
via Eq. (
26
).

When the intermediate configuration coincides with the current one, elastic moduli are designated as
\bar{\boldsymbol{C}}
, see Eq. (
34
).
These moduli also relate the Truesdell objective rate of the Cauchy stress and the deformation rate,

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nabla}}{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{Tr}=\bar{\boldsymbol{C}}:\boldsymbol{d}
.
For finite deviation of the intermediate configuration from the current one, one may need higher order elastic moduli in the intermediate configuration to evaluate energy and stresses.
